Girard Mimetic Theory

1The Straight Line Is a Lie2When the Model Lives Next Door3Wanting to Be Someone Else4Doubles5Take Away the Ranks6All Against One7Where the Verdict Can Be Checked8The Victim Becomes a God9How a Myth Hides Its Murder10Rules, Rites and the Substitute11Told From the Victim's Side12The God of the Crowd13The Crowd at the Cross14The Town That Took It Back15The Concern for Victims16Choose the Model

Cold open

Almost everything you want beyond food, warmth and sleep, you learned to want by watching someone else want it.1 That is why the people who get under your skin are almost never strangers.2 They are the brother, the colleague, the friend who did a little better. For most of human history, groups ended the violence this copying breeds by turning together on one person, and calling the peace that followed holy.3 On Girard's account, a story in which the crowd is wrong and the victim innocent has been slowly breaking that machine.4 That, he argued, is why our age is both the kindest to victims in history and crowded with accusers.5

1fact
Beyond basic bodily needs, desire is learned by watching another person's desire (mimetic desire).
René Girard, Violence and the Sacred, trans. Patrick Gregory (Johns Hopkins UP, 1977), ch. 6, "From Mimetic Desire to the Monstrous Double"

Put two small children in a room full of toys. One of them picks up a red truck. Within a minute the other one wants the red truck, and only the red truck. There are a dozen others on the floor, one of them identical. Nobody had to teach this. The truck was worth nothing until another child's hand closed on it. That scene holds the whole theory in small. The desire came from a model. The model became a rival. The object became precious because two people wanted it. Now make the two children adults, armed, in a town where everyone is doing the same. The scene no longer ends with a parent taking the truck away.

The theory belongs to René Girard. It is one of the few ideas in modern thought that runs without a break from the smallest act of envy to the founding of religions. It starts in the great novels, where the hidden model behind a character's desire is finally shown. It passes through the hatred between brothers and neighbours, and through the strange calm a crowd feels after it has driven someone out. It reads the oldest myths as the crowd's own account of such killings. And it ends with a claim about the story of the crucifixion. That claim is either the key to the modern world or an elaborate piece of special pleading. Some links in the chain have been measured, and a few of the measurements go against him. One link is a hypothesis that nobody can ever prove, and it stays a hypothesis.6

6fact
Part of the theory (the claim that cultures are founded on a hidden collective killing) is a hypothesis that cannot be proven, because the evidence that would test it is, on the theory's own logic, concealed by the culture it produced.
Burton L. Mack, "Introduction: Religion and Ritual," in Robert G. Hamerton-Kelly (ed.), Violent Origins: Walter Burkert, René Girard, and Jonathan Z. Smith on Ritual Killing and Cultural Formation (Stanford UP, 1987)
Mack, "Introduction: Religion and Ritual", p. 11
print copy
How it holds
Backs the cold open's one explicit admission that one part of the theory (the founding-murder hypothesis, developed in parts 6-7) is unfalsifiable in principle.
What it does not say
Mack is introducing the volume's debate, not himself attacking Girard; this is the honest statement of the method problem, not a refutation. None of it is comforting. It explains things most people would rather not see in themselves. The prize goes flat once it is won. Contempt is so often admiration in disguise.7 And joining a pile-on feels, for a day, like something got cleaned up. The chain ends with one choice, which the old machine used to make for us without our knowing. It begins with the belief that hides all of it, the belief that your desires are your own.

7fact
Contempt for a rival is frequently admiration that cannot admit itself.
René Girard, Mensonge romantique et vérité romanesque (Paris: Grasset, 1961)
ch. I, "Le désir « triangulaire »"

What it does not say
Girard's sentence is in French ("whoever hates first hates himself, because of the secret admiration his hatred conceals"); the script's English line is the show's own translation/compression, not a published English rendering. Part 1: The Straight Line Is a Lie

When you are hungry, you want food, and nobody had to show you how. The want comes from your body, it points at anything edible, and it stops when you have eaten. Now take a different want. A friend turns up in a jacket you had walked past for months without a glance. Within a week you are looking it up, comparing colours, wondering whether it would suit you. Nothing in your body changed. The jacket did not change either. The only new thing in the story is that someone you notice now wears it.

Those two wants feel the same from the inside, and that is the trouble. Both feel like yours. Both feel like a straight line running from you to the thing. The claim is that the second kind is not a line at all. Beyond the appetites of the body, every desire has three points. You, the thing you want, and the person whose wanting showed you it was worth wanting. That third person is the model. Take the model away, the claim runs, and the desire does not survive.

This is the first idea of René Girard, a French critic who spent his working life in American universities.8 He built one theory out of it, and followed it from the novel to the crowd to the cross. He found it first in fiction. And he found it there because novelists, at their best, could see in their characters what nobody sees in themselves.

8fact
René Girard was a French literary critic who spent his academic career teaching in the United States.
René Girard, The Girard Reader, ed. James G. Williams (New York: Crossroad, 1996), "René Girard: A Biographical Sketch" (by the editor)
pp. 1-2

René Girard, The Girard Reader, ed. James G. Williams (New York: Crossroad, 1996), "René Girard: A Biographical Sketch" (by the editor)
p. 2
print copy
How it holds
The editor's sketch records that Girard came to the United States in 1947 and then taught at Indiana, Duke, Bryn Mawr and Johns Hopkins.
What it does not say
The sketch was written in 1996, so "forty-nine years" counts to then; Girard taught at Stanford from 1981 until he retired, which the sketch also covers. Read in a digital copy of this printed edition; it is not linked because the free copies online are unauthorised uploads of an in-copyright book. The page number is the printed page of the Crossroad edition, read from the page markers of a digital copy and checked against the book's own contents page. A free borrowable scan of the same edition (free account) is at archive.org/details/girardreader0000gira/page/1.

The plainest case is the oldest one. A Spanish gentleman reads books of chivalry until he decides to become a knight. He takes a new name, Don Quixote, and rides out to find adventures. What matters is how he decides what to do once he is out there. He does not ask what he wants. He asks what Amadis of Gaul would do, the hero of his favourite romance. He says it outright, when he decides to go mad with grief in the mountains because Amadis once did. Amadis, he says, "was the polestar, day-star, sun of valiant and devoted knights", the one every knight is bound to copy.9 So when Quixote wants to fight a giant, the giant is not where the wanting started. Amadis fought giants. Girard puts it with no softening: Quixote no longer chooses the objects of his own desire, Amadis chooses them for him.10

9quotation
Don Quixote names Amadis of Gaul as the model every knight is bound to imitate.
Miguel de Cervantes, Don Quixote, trans. John Ormsby (1885), Part I, ch. XXV
Part I, ch. XXV
Read the passage
How it holds
This is the plainest textual statement of Quixote naming his own model, which Girard's theory treats as the clearest case of the triangle.
What it does not say
The script shortens Ormsby's sentence ("the one every knight is bound to copy" paraphrases "whom all we... are bound to imitate"); the quoted clause itself matches character for character.
10fact
Girard states that Don Quixote no longer chooses the objects of his own desire; Amadis chooses them for him.

ch. I, "Le désir « triangulaire »"
print copy
How it holds
States the founding example of the triangle in the exact form Girard gives it.
What it does not say
Girard's sentence is French; the script's English line is the show's own close translation, not the published Freccero English (which reads "Don Quixote has surrendered to Amadis the individual's fundamental prerogative"). Notice what this does to the giant. A windmill will do. The object barely matters, because the object was never the source. The source sits off to one side, in a book, and the knight's whole life runs through it.

The second case is a woman who never thought of herself as copying anyone. Emma Bovary grows up in a convent, where the girls pass round romantic novels in secret. Flaubert lists what was in them. "They were all love, lovers, sweethearts, persecuted ladies fainting in lonely pavilions".11 That is a list of scenes, and Emma grows up wanting the scenes. She marries a decent country doctor, and the marriage disappoints her almost at once. Nothing is wrong with the doctor. What is wrong is that he is not in the books. Her affairs, her debts, her taste in furniture and dresses all follow the same rule. She wants what she was shown, and she believes every time that the wanting is her own heart speaking.

11quotation
The convent novels Emma Bovary read as a girl were full of love, lovers and persecuted heroines.
Gustave Flaubert, Madame Bovary, trans. Eleanor Marx-Aveling, Part I, ch. VI
Part I, ch. VI
Read the passage
How it holds
Grounds the Emma Bovary case: her models are the convent novels, not a visible single person, which is the contrast with Quixote the script builds.
What it does not say
None beyond translation choice (Marx-Aveling).

Set the two side by side and the difference between them is the lesson. Quixote knows he is copying. He names his model and is proud of it. Emma does not know. Her model is a whole shelf of novels she half forgot, and she experiences what she learned there as her own nature. The triangle is the same in both. In one it is visible, and in the other it is hidden from the person living inside it.

Now strip the cases down. Leave out the knight, the convent and the century. There is a person, there is a thing, and there is someone else, near or far, real or invented, whose desire for the thing comes first. The person sees the other want, and wants. Then the person forgets the seeing and remembers only the wanting. The general claim is that one, with the details removed. Beyond appetite, desire takes its object from a model, and it hides the model from the one who desires. Quixote and Emma are the same sentence with different particulars filled in. Girard's word for this copying is mimetic, from the Greek for imitation, and mimetic desire means nothing more than copied desire.

Girard gave the common belief a name, because he wanted to mark it wrong. He called it the romantic lie. The romantic lie is the story that desire springs from inside a person, spontaneous and original, and runs straight to its object. It is the story of every love song that says nobody ever felt this before. It is also the story most of us tell about our own tastes. Against it he set what he called novelistic truth: the knowledge, found in a handful of great novels, that the model is always there. A romantic book, in his sense, is one that reflects the model's presence without ever revealing it.12 Emma's convent novels were romantic in exactly that sense, full of models and never admitting it. A great novel is one that shows the third point of the triangle.

12fact
Girard defines a "romantic" work as one that reflects the presence of the model without ever revealing it.
René Girard, Mensonge romantique et vérité romanesque (Paris: Grasset, 1961)
ch. I, "Le désir « triangulaire »"
print copy
How it holds
Defines the technical term "romantic" exactly as the plan uses it, in contrast to "novelistic".
What it does not say
The note that recorded this quote states explicitly that an English wording of this sentence was NOT found in The Girard Reader; the script's English line is the show's own translation of the French, not a published rendering. A free borrowable scan of the same edition (free account) is at archive.org/details/bwb_C0-ATA-988.

Here is the belief at its strongest, because it deserves that. People are different. One child loves music and another loves numbers before anyone has shown them either. Temperament is real, talent is real, and some tastes seem to arrive with a person.13 If desire were simply copied, everyone would want the same things, and they plainly do not. That is a good argument, and part of it stands. Temperament is real, and it decides which models a person finds attractive in the first place. What falls is the idea that the wanting itself comes from nowhere. The child who loves music still learned which music is worth loving, which instrument is serious and which is a toy, and whose praise counts. Differences in desire are real, and they are differences in which models a person picked up.

13fact
Temperament and talent differ between people from early life, partly through genes.
Hollowell et al., "Genome-wide association studies of infant and toddler temperament in European and multi-ancestry populations", Nature Human Behaviour (2026)
Abstract and Results
Read the passage
Mosing, Verweij, Hambrick, Pedersen, Ullén et al., "Testing the Deliberate Practice Theory: Does Practice Reduce the Heritability of Musical Expertise?", Journal of Intelligence 12 (2024)
Introduction
Read the passage
How it holds
Both studies find that early temperament and musical ability vary between people partly by inheritance, which is the strong form of the objection the script lets stand.
What it does not say
The genetic share of infant temperament measured from DNA is small (about 3 to 15 percent per trait); twin studies give larger figures. Neither study shows a liking for music or numbers before any exposure; that line is the script's illustration of the objection.

There is a line to draw here, and it matters for everything that follows. Hunger does not need a model. Thirst does not need one, nor cold, nor pain. The claim is not about any of them. But watch how quickly the model enters even there. Hunger tells you to eat. It does not tell you to want this restaurant, this bread, this way of eating that people you admire have made into a style. The appetite is yours. Almost everything you do with it is borrowed.

So the claim has a boundary, and it is a narrow one. The body supplies a few needs. Everything a person wants beyond those needs, Girard claims, comes through someone else first. That covers status, love of a particular kind, a career, a house in a certain street, and a self worth being.

The first objection anyone raises is that this is old news. Everyone knows about peer pressure, advertising and fashion. Social influence is a whole branch of psychology. Why give it a grand name? Because the two claims are different, and the difference is the whole theory. The ordinary picture of influence starts from a self that already wants things, which others then push, nudge or bend. Remove the advertising and the self is still there, with its own preferences intact. Girard's claim is that there is nothing underneath to push. Remove every model and you do not find a person with pure desires of their own. You find a person with appetites and no idea what to want. Influence, in the ordinary picture, is a pressure on desire. In Girard's picture it is where desire comes from. Those two claims predict different things. If others only push a desire you already had, a model you admire should leave you grateful. If the model is where the desire comes from, a model who comes close should become your rival.

The second objection is darker. If every desire is borrowed, then nothing I love is really mine. My vocation, my marriage, my tastes in books are all counterfeits. The theory sounds like a cynic's trick, a way of sneering at everyone's hopes. The answer begins with language. Every word you speak, you copied. You did not invent English, and nobody concludes that your sentences are fake. Copying is how a human being learns to do anything at all, from walking to wanting. A borrowed desire can be as deep and as lasting as any desire there is, because there is no other kind. What the theory takes away is one specific comfort: the belief that you chose your desires from some inner place that nobody else touched. It leaves a harder question in its place. If you are going to copy anyway, whose desires are you copying, and do you know?

Girard noticed something about Quixote that points ahead. He wrote that the knight's life is the imitation of Amadis in the same sense that the Christian's life is the imitation of Christ.14 He meant it as a comparison of structure, not as a joke at religion's expense. Both lives are organised around a model chosen on purpose and openly confessed. That comparison will come back near the end, when the question is no longer whether to imitate but whom.

René Girard, Deceit, Desire and the Novel, trans. Y. Freccero (Johns Hopkins UP, 1965), excerpted as "Triangular Desire" in The Girard Reader, ed. James G. Williams (Crossroad, 1996)
Girard Reader ch. 3, "Triangular Desire", p. 34
print copy

What it does not say
The script's "knight's life"/"Christian's life" lightly paraphrases Freccero's "Chivalric existence"/"Christian's existence"; no quote marks are used on air, so this is carried as fact, not quotation. So here is what stands at the close of the first step. Desire beyond appetite has three points, not two. The third point, the model, is visible in the knight because he boasts of it. It is hidden in Emma because she takes it for her own heart. The common belief that desire runs straight from a self to a thing is the romantic lie. It is wrong where it feels most certain. What is kept from it is temperament, which decides which models stick. What is thrown out is the idea of a want with no source. A knight who copies a hero from an old book harms windmills and himself. Nobody fights him for the giants. But most of the people whose desires we copy are not in books. They live in the same street, work in the same office, and want the same few things. Yet all that copying, which ought to make people agreeable, sets them against each other instead. The reason lies in how close the model stands.

Part 2: When the Model Lives Next Door

Copying a model who lives far away breeds no rivalry. Copying one who lives next door turns the model into a rival. The two of you are now reaching for the same thing with the same hand. And the closer the model comes, the harder the copier works to hide that he is copying, until admiration comes out the other side as contempt. That is the claim, and it explains a fact most people find embarrassing: the people who get under our skin are almost never strangers.

Start with two cases that differ on one thing only. Quixote copies Amadis, a knight from a book written long before he was born. Amadis cannot want anything Quixote wants, because Amadis is not in the world. Nothing Quixote reaches for is ever snatched away by his model. He can worship him all his life without a moment of resentment. Now put two colleagues in the same office. One of them admires the other, copies the way she talks in meetings, starts wanting the projects she wants. Then a promotion opens, and both want it. The admirer has learned to want it from the very person who now stands in the way.

The triangle is the same in both cases. A person, a model, a desired thing. What differs is distance. Not distance in miles, but distance in the world of things that can be had. Amadis and Quixote can never want the same windmill at the same time. The two colleagues can, and do, because they live in the same small world with the same few prizes.

Girard named the two cases, and the names are worth hearing once. He called the first external mediation, where, in his words, "the distance is sufficient to eliminate any contact between the two spheres of possibilities".15 He called the second internal mediation, where that distance shrinks enough for the two spheres to "penetrate each other".16 From here on the plain words will do: a far model and a near model.

15quotation
Girard defines external mediation as a distance between model and imitator sufficient to eliminate any contact between their two spheres of what is possible to have.

Here is the guess most people would make. The more you admire someone, the better you will get on with them. Admiration is a warm feeling, and warm feelings make for friends. The result runs the other way, and the theory says why. When the model is near, admiration and rivalry are the same act seen from two sides. To copy your neighbour's desire is to want what your neighbour wants, and what your neighbour wants is usually something only one of you can have. The admiration does not cool into rivalry over time. It is a rivalry from the start, waiting for the first object both people reach for.

Now the near model plays a double role. He shows you what to want, and then he stands between you and it. Girard puts it as a rule: the model cannot go on being a model without also becoming an obstacle.17 The person who taught you the desire is the person who blocks it. And that, he says, is what real hatred is. "Only someone who prevents us from satisfying a desire which he himself has inspired in us is truly an object of hatred".18

17fact
Girard holds that a mediator cannot keep being a model without also becoming, or seeming to become, an obstacle.
René Girard, Mensonge romantique et vérité romanesque (Paris: Grasset, 1961)
ch. I, "Le désir « triangulaire »"
print copy

That sentence repays a second hearing, because it explains why hatred is so personal. A stranger who takes the last seat on the train is an annoyance. A friend who gets the job you both wanted, the job you only wanted because you watched him want it, is something else. The stranger blocked a desire. The friend blocked a desire he gave you. The second injury is double, and so is the feeling it produces.

The clearest case in fiction is a provincial mayor in a novel by Stendhal. Monsieur de Rênal decides to hire a tutor for his children, a carpenter's son who knows his Latin. He has no great interest in his children's Latin. His reason, said to his wife in the opening chapters, is his rival in the town. The rival has fine horses, Rênal observes, "but he hasn't a tutor for his children". And then the worry that settles it. The rival "might take this one away from us".19 That is the whole mechanism in a few lines of dialogue. Nobody else has asked for the young man. There is no shortage of tutors. Rênal wants the young man because he imagines his rival wanting him, and the imagined wanting is enough. Later in the novel the rival does make a secret offer for the tutor, and the town talks of a "struggle for the clever young man".20 The guess had made itself true.

19quotation
Monsieur de Rênal's stated reasons for hiring the tutor are that his rival Valenod has fine horses but no tutor, and might take the tutor away from him.
Stendhal, The Red and the Black, trans. Horace B. Samuel, Book I, ch. 3
Book I, ch. 3

Girard draws the economic lesson from the scene. Rênal keeps raising what he will pay to keep the tutor. The rising price, Girard writes, "is determined by the imaginary desire which he attributes to his rival".21 The value of the thing is not in the thing. It is in the rival's eyes, or rather in what one man believes is in the other man's eyes. A price set by two neighbours watching each other has no ceiling except the one they reach together.

21quotation
Girard reads the rising price Rênal is willing to pay as set by the desire he imagines his rival Valenod has for the tutor.
René Girard, Deceit, Desire and the Novel, trans. Y. Freccero (Johns Hopkins UP, 1965), excerpted as "Triangular Desire" in The Girard Reader, ed. James G. Williams (Crossroad, 1996)

Now the most uncomfortable step. With a far model, the copier boasts. Quixote tells everyone who will listen that Amadis is his model. There is no shame in it, because nobody thinks a gentleman is competing with a hero from a book. Girard notes that the hero of the far model "proclaims aloud the true nature of his desire".22 With a near model, nobody boasts. Admitting that you want the promotion because your colleague wants it is admitting that she is above you, that you are her follower. So the copying goes underground. The copier insists the desire is his own, older than hers, better founded. And the admiration that started it all gets turned inside out.

22quotation
Girard holds that under external mediation the imitator openly proclaims the true nature of his desire.
René Girard, Deceit, Desire and the Novel, trans. Y. Freccero (Johns Hopkins UP, 1965), excerpted as "Triangular Desire" in The Girard Reader, ed. James G. Williams (Crossroad, 1996)
Girard Reader ch. 3, "Triangular Desire", p. 39

This is where contempt comes from. Girard writes that the one who hates first hates himself, for the secret admiration that his hatred hides.23 The near model must be run down, found ridiculous, found shallow, because the alternative is to admit that one has been following him. Envy and jealousy, he says, are "scarcely more than traditional names given to internal mediation".24 They are what copying feels like from the inside, once the copier can no longer admit to it.

The most extreme portrait of this belongs to Dostoevsky. His underground narrator is a minor clerk in the capital, proud and poor. In a tavern one evening an officer finds him in the way and takes him by the shoulders. He moves the clerk aside, and "passed by as though he had not noticed me".25 The clerk is destroyed. Not by the shove, but by not being noticed. For a long time afterwards he follows the officer, studies him, dreams of a duel, writes him a letter he never sends. He sees him on the main avenue, where the officer walks straight through the crowd and everyone steps aside, and he plans to not step aside. He borrows money for a better coat so that he can bump into the man as an equal. When he finally does it, the officer barely registers the collision, and the clerk goes home in triumph.26

25quotation
In Notes from Underground, an officer moves the narrator aside in a tavern and passes by as though he had not noticed him.
Fyodor Dostoevsky, Notes from Underground, trans. Constance Garnett, Part II ("Apropos of the Wet Snow"), ch. I

The note that recorded this scene flags that the fetch tool returned the specific "beaver collar" sentence with ellipses, and that it should be re-copied from the page directly before shipping; the plot detail is carried here as fact on that basis, not as a verbatim quotation of the coat sentence.

Read that story with the triangle in mind. The officer is not a model in the sense of a hero. The clerk despises him. But everything the clerk wants, he wants from the officer. He wants to be seen by him, to be as good as him, to walk the way he walks. The contempt and the copying are one passion. The coat, the walk and the bump are all borrowed from the man he claims to hate.

Now the claim with the mayor, the tutor, the clerk and the officer taken away. When a model is near enough to want what the copier wants, the model becomes a rival. The copier cannot admit the copying, so the admiration turns into resentment and contempt. And the value of the thing they fight over is set by each one's guess at the other's desire. The same structure runs under the tutor and the officer, which is why the same feeling runs under both.

Girard added one point about scale. As ranks fall and people become equals, models come closer. The near model, in his words, "triumphs in a universe where the differences between men are gradually erased".27 Envy is as old as anything human, but it was modern novels that made it their central subject.

27quotation
Girard holds that internal mediation (the near model) prevails where social differences between people are increasingly erased.
René Girard, Deceit, Desire and the Novel, trans. Y. Freccero (Johns Hopkins UP, 1965), excerpted as "Triangular Desire" in The Girard Reader, ed. James G. Williams (Crossroad, 1996)
Girard Reader ch. 3, "Triangular Desire", p. 42
print copy
How it holds
Backs the claim that equality, not distance, is what breeds rivalry.
What it does not say

The strongest objection deserves its full weight. Much rivalry is about scarce things, and needs no theory of copying at all. Two people want the same job because there is one job and both need the money. Two countries want the same river because there is one river and both need the water. Scarcity explains conflict without asking where anybody's desire came from. That is true, and Girard granted it: "it is true that the goods needed by human beings to sustain their lives can be scarce".28 What scarcity cannot explain are the rivalries that grow hottest where nothing material is scarce. Rênal did not need a tutor. The clerk did not need the officer's notice. The colleague could have wanted a dozen other projects. In each case the object became scarce at the moment two people wanted it, and became precious because they did. Rank and standing are scarce too, so these cases alone cannot choose between copying and plain competition for standing. So the verdict on scarcity is this. Scarcity starts some fights. Copying adds the escalation, and it runs under the fights over standing too. It also explains why so many of them are between people who look, from outside, almost exactly alike.

28quotation
Girard grants that the goods people need to live can genuinely be scarce.
René Girard, "Mimesis and Violence: Perspectives in Cultural Criticism" (orig. Berkshire Review 14, 1979), repr. as ch. 1 of The Girard Reader, ed. James G. Williams (Crossroad, 1996)
Girard Reader ch. 1, "Mimesis and Violence", p. 10
print copy
How it holds
Girard's own concession to the scarcity objection, used honestly here rather than invented.
What it does not say

So the second step is in place. A far model is worshipped in peace. A near model is copied in secret, resented in public, and turned into the obstacle to the very desire he gave. What the copier hides as contempt is admiration, and what he calls his own desire is his rival's desire guessed at and raised. But there is something strange in all three cases. Rênal does not care about Latin. The clerk cares nothing for the avenue or the coat. The prizes these men fight over are close to worthless to them. Which raises the question of what a rival is really after, if not the prize he is fighting for.

Part 3: Wanting to Be Someone Else

The prizes rivals fight over are often close to worthless to them, and that is a clue. At its deepest, a copied desire is not aimed at the object at all. It is aimed at the model, at what the model seems to have and the copier lacks: a fuller way of being a person. The object is a token of that self. And this explains a fact almost everyone has met and few can explain. The thing a person wanted most so often goes flat the moment it is theirs.

Start with the guess. Getting what you want should satisfy you. That is what wanting is for: you lack something, you get it, the lack is filled. For hunger it works that way. You eat, and the hunger goes. Now the result, for the other kind of want. The promotion comes through, and within a month the office feels the same. The house in the right street is bought, and the street turns out to be a street. The person you pursued for a year says yes, and something in you starts looking past them. The guess said satisfaction. The result is a strange emptiness, often followed by a new want aimed somewhere else.

Girard's account of the gap between the guess and the result is the centre of his theory of desire. A person whose basic needs are met, he wrote, still feels a lack, and the lack is not of any thing. "He desires being, something he himself lacks".29 And he believes some other person has it. The model seems to have it. The model seems to be more fully himself, more sure, more real. So the copier reaches for whatever the model reaches for, as if owning the same things would deliver the same self. That is why the prize goes flat. The object was supposed to carry the model's being across to the one who got it. It never does. The new owner is the same person as before, holding an object. Then the search moves on to the next model and the next object. Psychology has a plainer account of the flat prize, which is that people get used to whatever they own.30 That account is right as far as it goes. What it does not explain is why the next target is so often something another person wants.

29quotation
Girard holds that once basic needs are met, a person still feels a lack that is not of any particular thing, but of being, which he believes another person has.
René Girard, Violence and the Sacred, trans. Patrick Gregory (Johns Hopkins UP, 1977), ch. 6, "From Mimetic Desire to the Monstrous Double"
ch. 6, "From Mimetic Desire to the Monstrous Double"
print copy

What it does not say
The script quotes only the opening clause of Girard's sentence; it matches character for character. Girard drew a line here that matters for keeping the theory honest. He did not call every want desire in this full sense. He kept the word for the point where rivalry transforms "what was previously just an appetite or a need".31 The want gains a further dimension, a reach toward being. Hunger is still hunger. Appetite turns into this other thing when a meal becomes a statement about who someone is. Then another person's meal becomes a judgement on one's own.

31quotation
Girard reserves the word "desire" for the point where mimetic rivalry transforms what had been just an appetite or a need into something more.
René Girard, with Jean-Michel Oughourlian and Guy Lefort, Things Hidden Since the Foundation of the World, trans. Stephen Bann and Michael Metteer (Stanford UP, 1987), Book III ch. 1, "Mimetic Desire", section "From Object Rivalry to Metaphysical Desire"
Book III, ch. 1, p. 296 (speaker: R. G.)

What it does not say

The snob is the purest case, because snobbery is desire with the object almost removed. A snob, in Girard's description, does not dare to trust his own judgement, and wants only what others want.32 Take a club that will not let a certain man in. While it refuses, it is the most desirable room in the city. Its members seem to possess a way of being that he lacks. Then one day the letter comes and he is a member. Within a season the room is full of ordinary people, and he notices another club, one that has not let him in. Nothing about the first club changed. What changed is that he is now inside it, and he is still himself. The refusal was the source of the value, because the refusal was what proved the members were something he was not.

32fact
Girard describes the snob as someone who does not trust his own judgment and desires only what others desire.
René Girard, Mensonge romantique et vérité romanesque (Paris: Grasset, 1961)
ch. I, "Le désir « triangulaire »"
print copy
How it holds
Grounds the snob case as the purest form of desire aimed at being rather than any object.
What it does not say
Girard's sentence is general, about snobbery as such; the script's illustrative club scene that follows (a man excluded, then admitted, then bored) is an invented illustration of the general sentence, not itself drawn from a read Girard passage about a specific club or salon (the note explicitly flags that no Proust passage on Mme Verdurin or Legrandin was read). Read in a digital copy of this printed edition; it is not linked because the free copies online are unauthorised uploads of an in-copyright book. A free borrowable scan of the same edition (free account) is at archive.org/details/bwb_C0-ATA-988.

The snob waits outside a door. A stranger pair of cases tests the same idea from the other side. In each, a man who has the prize, or nearly has it, goes looking for a rival. There is a story told inside Don Quixote about a newly married man who goes to his closest friend and asks him to court his wife. He wants her "refined and tested by the fire of finding herself wooed".33 His reason, he says, is that "a woman is virtuous only in proportion as she is or is not tempted".34 The friend thinks at first it must be a joke, and then, pressed, he agrees.35 By the end all three are dead, and the husband's last note calls himself "the author of my own dishonour".36 In a short novel by Dostoevsky, a widower named Pavel is about to remarry, and he seeks out the man who was his dead wife's lover. He begs him to ride out with him and meet the bride's family.37 It is, he says, "merely an intense desire on my part and nothing more".38 He had even hired the carriage for the two of them before the lover said yes.39 Neither story states the triangle: the first husband speaks of testing virtue, and Pavel keeps his reasons to himself. But set them beside the snob. If desire ran straight from a man to his wife, both husbands would be making little sense. On the triangle they make a grim kind of sense. The prize has gone flat because it is owned, or nearly. The only way to make it shine again is to put another man's desire back on it. Each husband manufactures his own rival. Each husband gives a straight-line reason, or none, but the triangle predicts what they do, and two great writers recorded it.

Return now to the clerk who spent months planning to bump an officer on the avenue. He did it. He went home in triumph. And nothing in his life changed, because the collision was never the point. He wanted to be the kind of man who does not step aside, the kind of man the officer was. A shoulder against a shoulder cannot transfer that. And Emma Bovary, who wanted the love of the novels, found in her affairs what she had found in marriage. The lover became ordinary as soon as he was hers.40 The scenes she had been shown were never scenes anyone actually lives inside.

40fact
In Madame Bovary, Emma finds in adultery the same flatness she found in marriage.
Gustave Flaubert, Madame Bovary, trans. Eleanor Marx-Aveling
Part III, ch. 6
Read the passage
Gustave Flaubert, Madame Bovary (1857), French text
troisième partie, ch. VI
Read the passage
How it holds
Flaubert says it in one line: in adultery Emma found again all the platitudes of marriage.
What it does not say
The sentence is about her affair with Léon; the script generalises it to "her affairs".

Take away the office, the club, the avenue and the convent. A person sees a model who seems more fully alive. The person wants what the model wants, believing the thing will bring the self. The thing arrives and the self does not. The person moves on, still certain that the next thing is the right one. Each case is a reach for someone else's being, carried out through an object that cannot carry it.

Now the objection a careful listener should raise. This theory explains everything too easily. If you want something, you copied it. If you seem to want it for yourself, the model is merely hidden. If the prize satisfies you, it was an appetite, and if it does not, it was desire. A theory that can absorb any result tells you nothing. That objection lands on part of the theory, and it should be answered by evidence, not by cleverness. So here is what has been measured.

A team of brain scientists set out to test the simplest half of the claim. The question was whether seeing someone else want an object makes a person want it more. People watched short videos of the same objects. In some, a hand reached for the object as the goal of its action. In others, the object was there but nobody was after it. Then people rated how much they would like to have each object, on a scale from zero to ten. Here is the guess one might make in advance. Grown adults rating ordinary objects in a lab should be unmoved by a hand in a video. The result was that the objects someone had reached for were rated as more desirable. The effect was small. A repeat of the same task by the same group, a few years later, found it again, and smaller still.41 But it was there, and it went in the direction the theory predicts.

41statistic
In a lab study, people rated objects as slightly more desirable after seeing a hand reach for them as the goal of an action, on a 0-10 scale; the effect was small and was found again, smaller still, in a later replication by the same research group.
Maël Lebreton, Shiho Kawa, Baudouin Forgeot d'Arc, Jean Daunizeau, Mathias Pessiglione, "Your goal is mine: unraveling mimetic desires in the human brain", Journal of Neuroscience 32(21), 2012, 7146-7157
Materials and Methods; Results
Read the passage
Maël Lebreton, Shiho Kawa, Baudouin Forgeot d'Arc, Jean Daunizeau, Mathias Pessiglione, "Your goal is mine: unraveling mimetic desires in the human brain", Journal of Neuroscience 32(21), 2012, 7146-7157
Results
Read the passage
Baudouin Forgeot d'Arc et al., "Mimetic desire in autism spectrum disorder", Molecular Autism 7:45, 2016
Methods (tasks, participants) and Results
Read the passage
How it holds
This is the one place in the theory the script treats as directly measured, and it honestly reports the effect as small and shrinking on replication.
What it does not say
The 2012 study's effect is about half a point on a 0 to 10 scale (5.13 against 4.69, 116 subjects): real but small, and measured as ratings in a lab, not as purchases or rivalry. The 2016 study by the same group found the effect again in its control group, smaller.

What that result shows, and what it does not, needs saying. It shows that watching another person want a thing nudges what a person values, even in a bare lab with a hand on a screen. It does not show rivalry. It does not show that the nudge lasts, or that it grows into envy, or that people want the model's being. It is one measured step, the first one, and it came out the way the triangle says it should.

The deeper claim, that desire is finally a reach for another person's being, is an interpretation. It rests on the novels and on what anyone can check in their own life. It is a good one, because it explains why the moving target is so often someone else's target. And it could fail. Suppose people who won what their model wanted stayed content for years. Suppose a rival's arrival never raised a thing's value. Then the claim would be in trouble. Until then it is held as the best explanation of a pattern, not as a measured fact.

There is one more thing in this, and it concerns the novelists themselves. Girard noticed that the great novels of desire end in a similar way. Their heroes, at the close, give up the borrowed desire that ruled them. What they give up is what he called "metaphysical desire", his name for this desire for another person's being.42 And he argued that the novelists could only draw the triangle after they had seen it in themselves. It is a victory over this kind of desire, he wrote, that "transforms a romantic writer into a true novelist".43 This is the one place where Girard's own life shaped the theory, and it belongs here for that reason. In 1959 he was writing the last chapter of his first book, about the conversions at the end of these novels. He found that the description applied to him. "I realized I was undergoing my own version of the experience I was describing", he said later. He returned to the Christianity of his childhood that spring.44 The theory began as a reading of novels and became, for him, a reading of his own life.

42quotation
Girard's term for the desire the great novels' heroes give up at the end is "metaphysical desire".
René Girard, Deceit, Desire and the Novel, trans. Y. Freccero (Johns Hopkins UP, 1965), excerpted in The Girard Reader, ed. James G. Williams (Crossroad, 1996)
Girard Reader ch. 4, "Desire and the Unity of Novelistic Conclusions", p. 48

That completes the account of desire itself. A copied desire, at its deepest, wants the model's being, and uses objects as tokens of it. That is why the won prize goes flat and the target keeps moving. The simplest step of this has been measured, and the deeper one is an interpretation that explains what the straight line cannot. The novelists who saw it best saw it first in themselves. So far the copying has run one way, from the copier to the model. But a near model watches back. The next step is what happens when each of two people becomes the other's model, and both are reaching at once.

Part 4: Doubles

When two people copy each other's desire, something odd happens to the thing they are fighting over. It fades. Each rival starts copying the other's hostility instead, blow for blow and insult for insult. In the end, from the point of view of the fight, nothing tells them apart, though each insists to the last that they are opposites. That is the claim: violence grows out of sameness, not out of difference. And it is the reverse of what almost everyone believes about why people fight.

Put the belief first, as its holders would. People fight, on this view, because they are different. Different tribes, different gods, different interests, different natures. The cure for violence is understanding: learn about the other, find what you share, and the fighting stops. It is a generous view, and it fits the wars everyone learns about at school, between peoples who spoke different languages and prayed in different ways.

Now look at where people actually hate most hotly. Brothers. Neighbours over a fence. Two factions of the same party, two schools of the same religion, two firms in the same small market. Freud noticed it and gave it a name. He observed that "it is precisely communities with adjoining territories" that feud constantly and ridicule each other, the nearest neighbours rather than the distant foes. He called it the narcissism of minor differences. And then, honestly, he added that it was "a name which does not do much to explain it".45

45quotation
Freud observed that neighbouring communities are the ones locked in constant feuds and mutual ridicule, named this "the narcissism of minor differences", and admitted the name explained little.
Sigmund Freud, Civilization and Its Discontents, trans. James Strachey (Standard Edition vol. XXI / Norton 1961), ch. V
ch. V
Read the passage
Sigmund Freud, Civilization and Its Discontents, trans. James Strachey (Standard Edition vol. XXI / Norton 1961), ch. V
ch. V
Read the passage
How it holds

What it does not say

Freud's own explanation for the pattern is aggression seeking an outlet, not imitation; the script correctly treats him as an independent witness to the pattern, not as confirming Girard's cause. Read in Hanover College's excerpt of the Strachey translation, linked through an Internet Archive capture; the Standard Edition page was not checked.

Girard's theory is an attempt to supply the explanation Freud admitted he lacked. Start from where the last step ended. A copier wants what his near model wants, and the model becomes his rival. So far only one person is copying. But a model who finds himself opposed starts watching his imitator. The imitator's desire for the prize makes the prize look more precious to the model, who now wants it more. The copying has become mutual. In Girard's words, "each becomes the imitator of his own imitator and the model of his own model".46

46quotation
Girard holds that in mutual rivalry each person becomes the imitator of his own imitator and the model of his own model.
René Girard, "Mimesis and Violence: Perspectives in Cultural Criticism" (orig. Berkshire Review 14, 1979), repr. as ch. 1 of The Girard Reader, ed. James G. Williams (Crossroad, 1996)
Girard Reader ch. 1, "Mimesis and Violence", p. 9
print copy
How it holds
States the reciprocal-copying mechanism that turns one-way admiration into mutual rivalry.
What it does not say
9-19). Read in a digital copy of this printed edition; it is not linked because the free copies online are unauthorised uploads of an in-copyright book. The page number is the printed page of the Crossroad edition, read from the page markers of a digital copy and checked against the book's own contents page. A free borrowable scan of the same edition (free account) is at archive.org/details/girardreader0000gira/page/9.

That sentence describes a closed loop, and a closed loop has no brake. Each rival's desire is the other's reason to want harder. Every glance raises the stakes. And here is the turn. As the loop tightens, each rival is looking less at the prize and more at the other rival. The prize was only ever valuable because the other wanted it. So attention drifts from the object to the opponent, until, as Girard puts it, "the disputed object becomes secondary, even irrelevant".47

47quotation
Girard holds that as mutual rivalry intensifies, the disputed object becomes secondary, even irrelevant.
René Girard, "Mimesis and Violence: Perspectives in Cultural Criticism" (orig. Berkshire Review 14, 1979), repr. as ch. 1 of The Girard Reader, ed. James G. Williams (Crossroad, 1996)
Girard Reader ch. 1, "Mimesis and Violence", p. 13

Backs the segment's central claim that violence grows out of sameness, with the object dropping out.
What it does not say

The page number is the printed page of the Crossroad edition, read from the page markers of a digital copy and checked against the book's own contents page. Two neighbours fall out over a hedge. One cuts it lower, the other complains, the first plants something taller out of spite. Within a year the hedge is gone and the neighbours have stopped speaking. The quarrel is now about parking, children, a dog and a remark at a funeral. Ask either one what the fight is about and he will give a list, and the hedge will not be on it. The object has dropped out. What remains is two people answering each other.

A second case is barer still. Two drivers meet on a lane too narrow for both. Neither will reverse. Each copies the other's refusal, each inches forward when the other does, each leans on the horn when the other does. There is no road left to fight over, only the refusal itself. Nothing tells them apart except which way each car is facing. The neighbours and the drivers are one structure. Mutual copying first fixes two people on the same object, then fixes them on each other, and the object goes.

The oldest literature is full of this, and full of brothers. The two sons of Oedipus fight over the throne of Thebes, one inside the walls and one leading an army against them. Seven gates, seven champions, and at the seventh gate the king goes out himself to meet his brother. "Brother against brother, foe against foe, I will take my stand", he says.48 They kill each other. The chorus sings over them: "Dead are the men, by hands that slew their own".49 They fought for a kingdom, and each was given exactly as much of it as a grave needs. The prize was lost the moment the fight became about the brother.

48quotation
In Aeschylus's Seven Against Thebes, Eteocles says he will meet his brother Polynices at the seventh gate.

Girard noticed how often founding myths put two such figures at the centre of a crisis. They are, he writes, "generally conceived as close relatives, brothers, or identical twins".50 Cain and Abel, Romulus and Remus, the Theban brothers.51 He also noticed that many traditional societies treated real twins with fear. Two identical people in one place were treated as a danger to everyone around them.52 On his reading, the fear made a kind of sense, because the twin is a picture of what rivalry produces: two people nobody can tell apart.

Girard had a name for the rivals at the end of this process. He called them doubles. The word does not mean they are actually the same in every way. It means, in his careful phrasing, that "from the point of view of the antagonism, nothing distinguishes them".53 Each makes the same threats, the same accusations, the same claim to be the injured party. Each sees in the other the monster he himself is becoming.

53quotation
Girard calls mutual rivals "doubles" because, from the point of view of the antagonism, nothing distinguishes them.
René Girard, with Jean-Michel Oughourlian and Guy Lefort, Things Hidden Since the Foundation of the World, trans. Stephen Bann and Michael Metteer (Stanford UP, 1987), Book I ch. 1, section "Sacrifice and the Victimage Mechanism"
Book I, ch. 1, p. 26
print copy
How it holds
States the term the rest of the video uses for mutually escalating rivals.
What it does not say
The script's surrounding sentence ("The word does not mean they are actually the same in every way") correctly narrows the claim to how the rivals look from inside the fight, matching Girard's own "from the point of view of the antagonism" wording rather than claiming literal identity. Read in a digital copy of this printed edition; it is not linked because the free copies online are unauthorised uploads of an in-copyright book. The page number is the printed page, checked in a scan of this edition. A free borrowable scan of the same edition (free account) is at archive.org/details/thingshiddensinc0000gira/page/26.

And here is why the belief about difference gets the order backwards. The rivals do feel different. Each is certain the other is the aggressor, the cheat, the one who started it. But the feeling of difference is produced by the fight, not the cause of it. The closer the rivals become in what they do, the more fiercely each insists on the gulf between them. An outsider watching two factions tear each other apart over a doctrine sees two groups that are nearly identical. The factions see only the abyss. So the common view is wrong in a precise place. What it keeps is that difference is often the language of conflict, the story each side tells. What it loses is the claim that difference is the cause. Rivals come to want the same things, and the fight makes them more alike, while each tells himself the opposite.

Violence itself follows the same rule, and this is the step everything later hangs on. A blow invites a blow. An insult invites an insult. Violence spreads by copying as readily as anything humans do, because it is easy to copy and hard to refuse to copy. Girard is blunt about its status: "Violence is not originary; it is a by-product of mimetic rivalry".54 Mimetic rivalry, in his word, is just rivalry by copying. It is what copied desire becomes when two copiers block each other and keep copying.

54quotation
Girard holds that violence is not an original, separate act, but a by-product of mimetic rivalry.
René Girard, "Mimesis and Violence: Perspectives in Cultural Criticism" (orig. Berkshire Review 14, 1979), repr. as ch. 1 of The Girard Reader, ed. James G. Williams (Crossroad, 1996)
Girard Reader ch. 1, "Mimesis and Violence", p. 12
print copy
How it holds
States the thesis the rest of the part builds the Hobbes/Freud comparison against.
What it does not say
Read in a digital copy of this printed edition; it is not linked because the free copies online are unauthorised uploads of an in-copyright book. That is a real disagreement with Freud. Freud thought aggression was an inborn drive looking for an outlet, and the near neighbour was simply a convenient target. Girard thought aggression grew out of rivalry. For him the near neighbour was the natural target, because the near neighbour is the one who wants what you want. The two accounts predict different things. If aggression were only a drive looking for an outlet, any convenient target would do. Copied rivalry should hit hardest where wants overlap most. The brothers at the seventh gate, the neighbours over the hedge and the factions of one creed all fall where the second account predicts. On these cases the copying account fits better than an inborn drive, though plain competition for standing remains a rival account.

Once violence is copied, there is a further problem, which is revenge. Revenge is the copying of violence across time. Someone kills, and the victim's family kills back. The killer's family now has a death of its own to answer. Girard calls it "a chain reaction of vengeance".55 Every act of justice is, for the other side, a fresh injury. Nothing inside the logic of revenge ever says enough, because the last blow always belongs to someone who has not yet answered it.

55quotation
Girard calls the blood feud's spread across generations a chain reaction of vengeance.
René Girard, with Jean-Michel Oughourlian and Guy Lefort, Things Hidden Since the Foundation of the World, trans. Stephen Bann and Michael Metteer (Stanford UP, 1987), Book I ch. 1, "The Function of the Law: Prohibiting Imitation"
Book I, ch. 1, p. 12
print copy
How it holds
Supports the claim that revenge, once started, has no logic that says "enough".
What it does not say
A better-known formulation of this idea in Violence and the Sacred ("Vengeance... Read in a digital copy of this printed edition; it is not linked because the free copies online are unauthorised uploads of an in-copyright book. The page number is the printed page, checked in a scan of this edition. A free borrowable scan of the same edition (free account) is at archive.org/details/thingshiddensinc0000gira/page/12.

The weightiest reply comes from the philosopher of the war of all against all. Thomas Hobbes, writing in the middle of the English civil wars56, gave three causes of quarrel: competition, fear and glory. His first cause sounds like plain scarcity. "If any two men desire the same thing, which neverthelesse they cannot both enjoy, they become enemies".57 No copying needed, on this view. Two hungry men, one loaf, and fear of each other does the rest. There is real force in that. Hobbes grounds the two men's convergence in equal ability and similar passions, never in copying, and people with the same needs can collide without copying anyone.58 But his third cause, glory, has nothing to do with scarce goods. Hobbes says men will fight over trifles, a word, a smile, a different opinion, any sign of being undervalued.59 That is a fight over rank. Rank is scarce only because rivals make it so. Some first collisions need no copying, then, and what keeps these fights going, raises them and spreads them is the copying.

56fact
Hobbes wrote Leviathan (1651) amid the disorders of the English civil wars.
Thomas Hobbes, Leviathan (London: Andrew Crooke, 1651)
A Review, and Conclusion
Read the passage
How it holds
Hobbes himself ties the book, printed in 1651, to the disorders of his own time.
What it does not say
Hobbes says the book was occasioned by "the disorders of the present time"; the dates of the wars (1642-1651) are general history, not from this page.

Hobbes names competition, diffidence and glory as the three causes of quarrel, and states that if two men desire the same thing they cannot both enjoy, they become enemies.

That is how two people become doubles. The prize fades, the hostility is copied, and the violence passes down the generations. But two people are a small case. A family, a village or a city holds hundreds of pairs, all copying, all near, all wanting the same few things. If every pair in a community can turn into doubles at once, something has to hold the community together, and nothing obvious does.

Part 5: Take Away the Ranks

When rivalry spreads through a whole group, the group loses the differences that kept its members out of each other's way. Everyone becomes a near model to everyone, and every pair can turn into doubles. The violence passes from pair to pair the way a fire passes from house to house. Girard called this the crisis, and his claim about it is severe. Once it starts, nothing inside the group can be relied on to stop it.60

60fact
Girard calls the spread of rivalry through a whole group a (mimetic) crisis, in which once it starts nothing inside the group can be relied on to stop it.
Rene Girard, "Mimesis and Violence" (orig. Berkshire Review 14, 1979), repr. as ch. 1 of The Girard Reader, ed. James G. Williams (Crossroad, 1996)
Girard Reader ch. 1, "Mimesis and Violence", p. 11
print copy
How it holds
The passage is Girard's description of the mimetic crisis the video names here.
What it does not say
Girard's text does not independently state that nothing inside the group can stop the crisis once started; that is the video's own gloss on the dissolution-of-differences passage. Read in a digital copy of this printed edition; it is not linked because the free copies online are unauthorised uploads of an in-copyright book. The page number is the printed page of the Crossroad edition, read from the page markers of a digital copy and checked against the book's own contents page. A free borrowable scan of the same edition (free account) is at archive.org/details/girardreader0000gira/page/11.

The best description of it was written four centuries ago, in one of Shakespeare's less often staged plays. The Greek army has been camped outside Troy for years and cannot take the city. The commanders hold a council to ask why. Ulysses, the cleverest of them, gives an answer that has nothing to do with walls or numbers. The army is losing, he says, because nobody keeps his place. Their greatest fighter lies in his tent mocking his general, and the men below copy his contempt, each despising the one just above him. Then Ulysses says what happens when that spreads. "Take but degree away, untune that string, and hark what discord follows".61 Degree means rank, the order of who stands where. Without it, he goes on, strength becomes right. Appetite, which he calls a universal wolf, eats everything in reach and at the last eats up itself.62

61quotation
Ulysses tells the Greek commanders: "Take but degree away, untune that string, and hark what discord follows."
William Shakespeare, Troilus and Cressida, Act I scene 3 (Globe-based text, The Complete Works of William Shakespeare, MIT)

What it does not say
This edition punctuates "And, hark, what discord follows!"; the words are the same as spoken. The speech is Ulysses's, a character arguing a case, not Shakespeare speaking.

Ulysses's speech says that without degree, power becomes will and appetite becomes a universal wolf that eats everything and finally itself.

What it does not say

Hear the speech the way Girard heard it, as a piece of social science rather than a defence of kings.63 The army has not split into two camps with a clear quarrel. It has dissolved. Every man is measuring himself against the man nearest him, copying his scorn for the man above, wanting his place. The ranks were doing one job, which was to keep people from being each other's models. A soldier does not compete with a general, so he can admire him safely. Take that away and everyone is near to everyone.

63fact
Girard heard Ulysses's degree speech as a piece of social science about the dissolution of distinctions, not a defence of kings.
René Girard, Violence and the Sacred, trans. Patrick Gregory (Baltimore: Johns Hopkins UP, 1977)
ch. 2, "The Sacrificial Crisis", on Ulysses's speech in Troilus and Cressida
print copy
René Girard, Violence and the Sacred, trans. Patrick Gregory (Baltimore: Johns Hopkins UP, 1977)
ch. 2, "The Sacrificial Crisis"
print copy
How it holds
Girard refuses the "great chain of being" reading and treats the speech as an account of what happens to a society when its distinctions collapse.
What it does not say
This is Girard's reading in Violence and the Sacred, not his later book on Shakespeare, which was not read. Read in a digital copy of this printed edition; it is not linked because the free copies online are unauthorised uploads of an in-copyright book. A free borrowable scan of the same edition (free account) is at archive.org/details/violencesacred0000gira.

An army is too big to watch. Shakespeare also drew the same crisis small, in a comedy about four young people lost in a wood. A Midsummer Night's Dream begins with two young men, Lysander and Demetrius, who are as alike as the play can make them. Lysander tells the duke so himself: "I am, my lord, as well derived as he, As well possess'd". In the same breath he claims the one difference he has left: "my love is more than his".64 Equal in birth and in wealth, each insists he is not the other, which is the double from the last step, speaking. Before any magic enters the play, desire has already jumped. Demetrius had courted a girl named Helena and won her. Then he dropped her for Hermia, the very woman his equal loves.65 The play gives no reason for the switch; the one thing it shows is where Hermia stands, at the centre of another man's desire. Then comes the wood, and a flower whose juice makes a sleeper love the first face he sees. Both young men are dosed, and both wake loving Helena.66 The two women, who grew up "Like to a double cherry, seeming parted", turn on each other, and the two men go off to fight.67 In one night the four have become a small crowd of rivals, each against each, at the edge of blood. In the plot the flower does the night's work, and the first man dosed even falls for a woman nobody wanted. But the first act has already shown a man's love leap to his double's beloved with no flower at all. And the knot is finally cut from above. Hermia's father has begged the duke for "the law, the law" against Lysander, and the duke answers him, "I will overbear your will".68 The order that sends the four home in pairs comes from a power standing above all of them.

64quotation
In A Midsummer Night's Dream, Lysander tells Duke Theseus he is as well born and as wealthy as his rival Demetrius, and that his love exceeds his rival's.
William Shakespeare, A Midsummer Night's Dream (Project Gutenberg e-text #1514)
Act I, sc. 1 (Lysander to Theseus)

Girard described the same thing in his own terms. He wrote of "a mimetic crisis in which the differences that constitute the society are dissolved".69 At its height, he said, the distinctions that give each member a place "are merged into a communal mass".70 The structure is the one from the last two steps, scaled up. Two rivals become doubles. A group full of rivals becomes a crowd of doubles, all making the same accusations against each other.

There are two everyday versions worth holding beside the play. A family business loses its founder. While she lived, nobody doubted who decided, and her children could each admire her without competing with her. The week after the funeral, every sibling is suddenly a possible head of the firm. Each one's ambition teaches the others to want the same chair. Within a year the family is in court. Or take a team whose manager leaves and is not replaced. The work was going well. But now every decision is a contest between equals, and the contests start to be about who wins rather than what gets decided. Nothing material has changed in either case. A difference that kept people apart has gone, and copying fills the gap.

This needs one guard, because it is easy to hear as a defence of hierarchy. It is not one. Girard's point was never that kings are good. It was that a world with fixed ranks has fewer near models, and so fewer chances for desire to collide. The ranks bought that peace at a price that is obvious, and a world of equals has many goods the old order never had. The claim is narrower, and it is about cost. Equality brings models closer, and close models are what turn admiration into rivalry. On his view, every gain in equality is also a widening of the field of rivals.

Now add the step that makes the crisis dangerous. Violence is copied. Once one pair comes to blows, the blows invite answers, and the answers come from relatives and friends who were not in the first quarrel. Girard notes that in a feud the duty of revenge falls on "distant relatives who had nothing to do with the original act". The violence does not stay with the doubles who began it. It spreads through every tie they have.71 A quarrel between two men becomes a quarrel between two families, and then two clans. In the end a whole valley is divided by killings whose first cause nobody can name.

71quotation
Girard notes that in a feud, the duty of revenge falls on "distant relatives who had nothing to do with the original act," so the violence spreads through every tie the doubles have.
Rene Girard with J.-M. Oughourlian and G. Lefort, Things Hidden Since the Foundation of the World, trans. Stephen Bann and Michael Metteer (Stanford UP, 1987), Book I ch. 1, "The Function of the Law: Prohibiting Imitation"
Book I, ch. 1, p. 12
print copy
How it holds
Supports the feud-spreading claim with Girard's own words.
What it does not say
The claim that it "spreads through every tie they have" is the video's own gloss on the same note's related sentence, "it moves from generation to generation." Read in a digital copy of this printed edition; it is not linked because the free copies online are unauthorised uploads of an in-copyright book. The page number is the printed page, checked in a scan of this edition. A free borrowable scan of the same edition (free account) is at archive.org/details/thingshiddensinc0000gira/page/12.

Why does nothing stop it from inside? Because every possible brake requires both sides to stop at once. A truce needs both families to lay down arms on the same day. A payment of blood money needs the injured side to accept that the account is closed. But doubles are defined by the fact that each sees himself as the injured party and the other as the aggressor. Each side can always point to an unanswered blow. So feuding peoples had brakes, and the brakes were fragile, because they depended on the very agreement the crisis destroys.

The modern world has a brake that does not need both sides to agree, and it is so familiar that it is hard to see. It is the court. When a man is killed today, his family does not kill the killer's brother. The state tries him and punishes him, and the punishment is the end of the matter. Nobody in the killer's family is entitled to answer it. Girard puts the difference in one phrase: the court has "the power of a judicial institution that transcends all antagonists".72 That is what makes it work. The court stands above both families, so its blow is not a move in the feud. It is the end of the feud. It turns a killing into a crime, something judged from outside, instead of an injury to be paid back from inside. It may also be why the fall of ranks did not bring more killing. Historians of violence find killing falling over the same centuries in which courts grew strong.73

72quotation
Girard puts the difference between a feud and a court in one phrase: the court has "the power of a judicial institution that transcends all antagonists."

p. 628-629, section "The Main Patterns and Secular Trends"; decisive turn dated to "the early seventeenth century" (p. 629)

How it holds
Supports the claim that the long decline in killing tracks the rise of strong courts, with the correlational and data-quality caveats named.
What it does not say
Eisner's data measure homicide only, in Europe only, from coroners' and court records, not war deaths, and he himself cautions "the quantitative data discussed above should not be regarded as precise measurements." The decline is correlational with the strengthening of courts and the state, not shown by Eisner to be caused by it. A critique of the series (Schwerhoff et al. 2021) exists and was not read (see research/parts-10-12.md). The link is a copy of the article as published in the British Journal of Criminology, kept on a third-party academic site; no copy on the journal's or author's own site was reachable.

Most people would say courts exist to find out who is guilty. That is one of their jobs. But a court that found the guilty party and left the punishment to the victim's family would solve nothing. The family would punish, and the punished family would answer. The court's deepest job is to have the last word, a violence that nobody may copy back. Girard's formula for it: under the public system, "an act of vengeance is no longer avenged".74 Finding the truth is what makes that last word bearable, but the last word is what stops the chain.

74quotation
Under the public system, Girard's formula is "an act of vengeance is no longer avenged."
Rene Girard, Violence and the Sacred, trans. Patrick Gregory (Johns Hopkins UP, 1977), ch. 1 "Sacrifice", repr. as ch. 6 "Sacrifice as Sacral Violence and Substitution" in The Girard Reader (Crossroad, 1996)
Girard Reader ch. 6, "Sacrifice as Sacral Violence and Substitution", p. 85
print copy
How it holds
Supports the claim that the court's distinctive power is that its act of vengeance cannot itself be avenged, ending the chain.
What it does not say
Read in a digital copy of this printed edition; it is not linked because the free copies online are unauthorised uploads of an in-copyright book. The page number is the printed page of the Crossroad edition, read from the page markers of a digital copy and checked against the book's own contents page. A free borrowable scan of the same edition (free account) is at archive.org/details/girardreader0000gira/page/85.

So here is what stands. Rivalry in a whole group dissolves the differences that kept people out of each other's way, until everyone is a near model to everyone. Violence spreads along every tie, because it is copied. The group's own brakes fail, because they need both sides to stop together, and doubles never do. The modern answer is a power above both sides whose blow nobody answers. But courts and states are recent. For most of the time humans have lived in groups, there was no power standing above the families, and still groups survived their crises.75 Something stopped the fire. What a group in that state actually did, before there were courts, is the darkest step in the whole chain.

75fact
Girard, citing ethnologists, holds that primitive societies had no judicial system above the parties.
René Girard, The Girard Reader, ed. James G. Williams (New York: Crossroad, 1996), ch. 6 (from Violence and the Sacred, ch. 1)
p. 85
print copy
How it holds
Girard states that societies without courts are the ethnological norm, and his whole argument is about how they survived without them.
What it does not say
This is Girard's claim, resting on Malinowski and Radcliffe-Brown, whom he quotes; "most of the time humans have lived in groups" is the script's framing of it. Read in a digital copy of this printed edition; it is not linked because the free copies online are unauthorised uploads of an in-copyright book.
