A free borrowable scan of the same edition (free account) is at archive.org/details/girardreader0000gira/page/85.

Part 6: All Against One

When everyone in a group is copying everyone, they do not only copy desires. They copy accusations. And an accusation is the easiest thing in the world to copy, because it costs nothing and promises relief. So the war of all against all turns, by the same contagion that started it, into a war of all against one. That is the claim. And the most disturbing part of it, on Girard's account, is that when the one is killed or driven out, the peace that follows is real.

Follow the mechanism one step at a time, because each step is small. The group is in crisis, every pair of rivals accusing the other. The accusations point in a hundred directions, and none of them can win, because every accuser is also accused. Then one accusation gets a little more agreement than the others. Perhaps the man is odd, or new, or simply standing nearest. The people near the accusers copy them, as people copy everything. A second voice joins, then a fifth, then a crowd. Girard describes the snowball precisely. "More and more individuals polarize against fewer and fewer enemies until, in the end, only one is left".76 The hostility that was scattered across a hundred quarrels now runs in one direction. And then the one is struck, by everyone.

76quotation
Girard describes the contagion: "More and more individuals polarize against fewer and fewer enemies until, in the end, only one is left."
Rene Girard, "Are the Gospels Mythical?", First Things 62 (April 1996), 27-31
section on the mimetic cycle (web text, no page numbers)
Read the passage
How it holds
Direct quotation, exact match.
What it does not say
The link is the essay as First Things published it; that is its first publication, so the page is Girard's own text, not a summary of it.

Here is the step that makes the whole thing work. A killing by everyone is a killing nobody avenges. Recall the feud, where every blow called for an answer because each side had someone left to strike back. A victim struck by the whole group has no one left on his side. The chain of revenge has nowhere to go. And all the rage that a hundred rivals were spending on each other has been spent on one person at once. The rivals stand together over the body, no longer rivals. A quarrelling office that unites against one outsider shows a small version of it. The quarrels stop, because everyone is suddenly on the same side. In Girard's words, "Scandals evaporate and peace returns". He adds, pointedly, that it lasts only for a while.77

77quotation
In Girard's words, "Scandals evaporate and peace returns" -- for a while.
Rene Girard, "Are the Gospels Mythical?", First Things 62 (April 1996), 27-31
section on the mimetic cycle (web text, no page numbers)
Read the passage
How it holds
Direct quotation of the first clause; the dash-clause is paraphrased rather than quoted a second time.
What it does not say
The script's "he adds, pointedly, that it lasts only for a while" is a paraphrase of the same sentence's own "for a while", not a separate quotation. The link is the essay as First Things published it; that is its first publication, so the page is Girard's own text, not a summary of it.

That condition can be tested from the other side, by a killing that was meant as a sacrifice and was not done by everyone. The murder of Julius Caesar is told by an ancient historian and staged by Shakespeare, and both tell it in the language of the altar. The historian says every one of them had to strike, "for all had to take part in the sacrifice and taste of the slaughter".78 In the play, their leader Brutus says it outright: "Let us be sacrificers, but not butchers".79 When another conspirator proposes killing Caesar's closest ally as well, Brutus refuses, because "Our course will seem too bloody".80 So the killing is shared by every killer, and by nobody else. The city has not joined in. And the crowd turned on the killers. In the historian's account it burns the body in the forum, and then "ran to the houses of the murderers with intent to burn them down".81 A friend of Caesar named Cinna, who had come to the burning, is caught in the crowd. One of the conspirators had the same name, and "word ran through the whole throng that this man was one of the murderers of Caesar". "The crowd rushed upon him and tore him in pieces among them".82 In his life of Brutus, the same historian adds that this Cinna was "a poet, who had no share in the crime".83 Shakespeare keeps the scene and makes it unbearable. "I am Cinna the poet, I am Cinna the poet", the man cries. A voice in the crowd answers, "Tear him for his bad verses". Another says, "It is no matter, his name's Cinna".84 Two results follow for the step. A sacrifice performed by a faction gets avenged. Ordinary politics predicts the same, since the city loved Caesar, so the case fits the condition rather than proving it. The killers wanted a death that ends things, and because they were not everyone, the city came after them. Unanimity is the condition, not a detail. And the crowd that came after them chose its first victim by a name, without asking what the man had done.

78quotation
Plutarch records that all of Caesar's killers had to take part in the killing, in the standard English translation calling it a sacrifice.
Plutarch, Life of Caesar, trans. Bernadotte Perrin (Loeb Classical Library, 1919)
ch. 66, sect. 6
Read the passage
How it holds
Opens the new Part 6 fold: a killing meant as a sacrifice, carried out by a faction rather than by everyone, tested against the unanimity condition.
What it does not say
"Sacrifice" is Perrin's English for Plutarch's Greek, not confirmed as the only possible rendering; say "in the standard translation" rather than treating the word as Plutarch's own unmediated choice. Plutarch does not say the conspirators "agreed" to this in these words; say only that Plutarch records all of them had to take part.

The case Girard opened with is a poem, and how he used it matters. In the middle of the fourteenth century the plague killed a vast share of Europe. A celebrated French poet and composer, Guillaume de Machaut, wrote a long poem in those years. In it he reports, as plain fact, that the Jews had poisoned the water.85 In Girard's translation of the passage, they "poisoned several rivers and fountains that had been clear and pure so that many lost their lives".86 Machaut goes on to report that the Jews were killed for it.87 Every modern reader knows at once that the Jews did not poison the rivers. And Girard's question is how we know. Nobody has the water to test.88 Machaut was not lying, either; as Girard puts it, "Guillaume did not invent a single thing".89 He believed what he wrote. Yet we read his poem, reject its meaning entirely, and are certain that we are right to.

Guillaume de Machaut, a celebrated French poet and composer, wrote a long poem in the plague years reporting as fact that the Jews had poisoned the water.
Guillaume de Machaut, Le Jugement dou Roy de Navarre, in Oeuvres, ed. Ernest Hoepffner, t. I (Paris: Firmin-Didot, SATF, 1908), pp. 136-282

We know because the poem has a shape, and the shape gives it away. Girard named four marks of what he called texts of persecution. He called them stereotypes, meaning features that recur in every such account, and they can be said as four plain steps. One, a crisis, in which order collapses and the differences between people dissolve. The plague did that: rich and poor died alike, and every rule of ordinary life broke down.90 Two, an accusation of a crime that attacks the foundations of order. In Girard's phrase, such crimes "attack the very foundation of cultural order, the family and the hierarchical differences".91 Poisoning the common water, incest, killing a father or a king are the classic charges. They are crimes big enough to explain a catastrophe. Three, a victim chosen by signs rather than by evidence, the way the poet was chosen for his name. Foreigners, cripples, the very old, minorities, and at the other extreme, kings. "Ethnic and religious minorities tend to polarize the majorities against themselves", Girard wrote.92 The signs are anything that sets a person a little apart, and so makes the crowd's choice feel natural. "The fourth stereotype is violence itself". The crowd kills or expels.93 When all four appear together, a reader knows what happened, whatever the text says happened. The crisis was real. The crime was invented. The victim was chosen for his signs. And the violence was real.

90fact
The first mark of a persecution text is a crisis in which order collapses and the differences between people dissolve; the plague produced that.
Rene Girard, The Scapegoat (Johns Hopkins UP, 1986), ch. 2 "Stereotypes of Persecution"
ch. 2, p. 12

Now the belief this overturns. Scapegoating, on the common view, is a mistake of ignorant people. They did not understand disease, so they blamed the nearest stranger. Give them the facts, and the blaming stops. It is a hopeful view, and it fits our sense that we, who know about germs, are past all that. Here is a fact that tests it. In 1348, while the killings spread, the pope himself issued a bull defending the Jews. He pointed out that the plague was killing people even "where these same Jews have not lived", so the Jews could not be its cause.94 That is a perfectly good argument from evidence, given by the highest authority Machaut's world recognised. The killings went on. So the common view fails at one point. It is right that the accusation is false. It is wrong to think that the crowd believes it because of a gap in its knowledge. The crowd believes it partly because everyone around them believes it, and partly because believing it seems to work. The peace is the evidence the crowd trusts.

94quotation
In 1348 the pope issued a bull defending the Jews, pointing out the plague killed people even "where these same Jews have not lived."
Clement VI, bull defending the Jews, Avignon, 26 September 1348; English translation as posted by the Center for Online Judaic Studies (translator not credited)
the bull's central sentence
Read the passage
How it holds
Supports the claim that the highest authority of the time gave evidence against the accusation, and the killings continued anyway.
What it does not say
The bull's name ("Quamvis perfidiam Judaeorum") is from memory and unverified, so is not voiced. A published translation to cite alongside: R. Horrox (ed. and trans.), The Black Death (Manchester UP, 1994), pp. 221-222 (not read directly; page from Jedwab, Johnson & Koyama p. 22). The page carries a full English translation of the bull; the translator is not named.

That is a strong claim, and claims like it can be tested. If crises raise the odds of a victim, then in measured history, bad times should bring more persecution. Four tests are worth hearing, and they do not all agree. The first is witch trials in early modern Europe. An economist set the number of trials in eleven regions across two and a half centuries against the weather. The guess is that the two would have nothing to do with each other. The result was that colder decades, the worst years of a little ice age that ruined harvests, brought more trials. Weather explained only a modest part of the variation, but the direction was clear.95 The second is closer to our own time. In villages in rural Tanzania, in the years around 2000, people accused of witchcraft were still being killed. An economist counted the killings over ten years in sixty-seven villages, and set them against rainfall. In years of drought or flood, witch killings doubled. Other murders did not rise. Nearly all the victims were old women, usually killed by their own relatives.96

95statistic
Across 11 European regions over about two and a half centuries, colder decades brought more witch trials; weather explained only a modest part of the variation, but the direction was clear.
Emily Oster, "Witchcraft, Weather and Economic Growth in Renaissance Europe", Journal of Economic Perspectives 18(1), 2004, 215-228 (author's manuscript dated Jan. 8, 2004)
manuscript pp. 7-9 and Table 1 (p. 22)
Read the passage
How it holds
Grounds the statistic exactly as the study reports it, including the modest size of the effect.
What it does not say
The study measures trial counts from secondary sources across 11 regions, standardised and detrended; it does not measure persecutions outside Europe or non-witch violence. The link is an Internet Archive capture of the author's own manuscript on her website; the published Journal of Economic Perspectives text (2004) is free at aeaweb.org but refuses scripted visits.
96statistic
In 67 rural Tanzanian villages around 2000, witch killings doubled in years of extreme rainfall (drought or flood); other murders did not rise; victims were nearly all elderly women, usually killed by relatives.
Edward Miguel, "Poverty and Witch Killing", Review of Economic Studies 72(4), 2005, 1153-1172
p. 1153 (introduction/abstract), p. 1162 (96% female), p. 1166 (Table 4), p. 1170 (non-witch murders)
Read the passage
Edward Miguel, "Poverty and Witch Killing", Review of Economic Studies 72(4), 2005, 1153-1172 (secondary capture via Wayback)
p. 1153 abstract
Read the passage
How it holds
Grounds the statistic and its qualifiers exactly as the study reports them.
What it does not say
The study covers 67 villages in Meatu District, Shinyanga Region, Tanzania, 1992-2002; "doubled" is the study's own phrase for the rainfall-shock effect on witch murders specifically, not on murders overall. Victims are 96% female; disease epidemics (also locally blamed on witches) did not raise killings, which the study's author uses to argue for an economic rather than purely blame-driven mechanism the author treats this as economic rather than purely blame-driven.

That second study deserves a careful reading, because it cuts in two directions. It fits the form Girard described almost too well: a crisis, an accusation of witchcraft, a victim marked by age and sex. But the economist who ran it gave a different explanation. When food runs out, a desperate household gives up the member whose future earnings are lowest. And he tested the scapegoat reading directly. Outbreaks of disease, which people there also blamed on witches, did not raise the killings at all. Only hunger did.97 So in this case the accusation looks like the language in which a household's hunger is spoken, and not like a crowd's search for a cause.

97fact
Miguel's own explanation for the Tanzania result is economic: when food is short, a household gives up the member with the lowest future earnings; disease outbreaks, also blamed on witches, did not raise killings, only hunger did.
Edward Miguel, "Poverty and Witch Killing", Review of Economic Studies 72(4), 2005, 1153-1172
pp. 1157-1158 (mechanism), p. 1154 (disease-epidemic test)
Read the passage
How it holds
Supports the claim that the data cuts against a pure scapegoating explanation, as the script's own honest framing states.
What it does not say
Miguel himself hedges: "it is difficult to definitively disentangle this income shock theory from alternative socio-cultural explanations" (p. 1154), so the economic reading is the author's preferred one, not a disproof of the scapegoat reading.

The third test is the most famous, and it has weakened badly. In 1940 two American psychologists reported that lynchings in the American South rose when the cotton economy fell. It became a textbook example of frustration turning into violence against a scapegoat.98 When later researchers redid the analysis with modern methods, the link was modest. And when they extended the data into the Great Depression, the worst economic collapse of the century, the link nearly vanished. Real income fell sharply in the first years of the crash, and lynchings fell too.99 The fourth test returns to the plague and Machaut's victims. A recent study of over a hundred towns in the German lands found that almost half of them persecuted their Jews. The guess is that the worst-hit towns would persecute most. The result was the reverse, beyond a point. Persecution was likeliest where around one person in five died, and grew rarer as the death toll climbed higher. What predicted it best was older: a town's history of hatred toward Jews, and whether the Jews there were economically needed.100

98fact
In 1940 two American psychologists reported that lynchings in the American South rose when the cotton economy fell, taken as a textbook example of displaced frustration-aggression.
Carl I. Hovland and Robert R. Sears, "Minor Studies of Aggression: VI. Correlation of Lynchings with Economic Indices", Journal of Psychology 9 (1940), 301-310
pp. 306-308 (excerpt card)
Read the passage
How it holds
Grounds the fact of the 1940 study and its conclusion sentence, without a specific statistic.
What it does not say
Only a two-page excerpt was read; the correlation coefficients are not in the excerpt and must not be voiced. The link is a facsimile of the article's own printed pages (pp. 306-308) kept by the Erich Fromm archive.

100statistic
A study of over a hundred German-lands towns found almost half persecuted their Jews during the Black Death; persecution was likeliest around 20% mortality and grew rarer at higher death tolls; the best predictors were older antisemitism and economic need for Jews, not the raw death toll.
Remi Jedwab, Noel D. Johnson and Mark Koyama, "Negative Shocks and Mass Persecutions: Evidence from the Black Death", MPRA Paper 77720 (March 2017); published as Journal of Economic Growth 24(4), 2019, 345-395 (bibliographic record only, not read)

What it does not say
"58 out of the 124 towns -- or 46.8% of the sample -- saw a persecution" is the working paper's own baseline count (close to the script's "almost half"). The link is the authors' own working paper in the University of Munich repository.

Count them up. One test points Girard's way, one points against him, and two are mixed. So here is the ruling, and it has to cover both halves. Crisis does raise the odds of a victim in some measured cases, and the victims carry the kinds of signs Girard named. That much holds. But crisis is not a switch that produces a lynching. Hunger, money, old hatreds and simple calculation run alongside the copying, and sometimes they do most of the work. The scapegoat mechanism is a tendency with a known shape, not a law of history. And none of these studies measures the calm afterwards. That the peace follows is Girard's inference from the mechanism, not a recorded result. Its strongest evidence is the shape itself. The four marks let a modern reader see through a persecutor's own account, with no outside evidence at all.

So the sixth step is in place. In a crisis, accusation spreads by copying, until a whole group agrees on one victim. The victim is chosen by marks, and charged with a crime big enough to explain the disaster. A killing by everyone is avenged by no one, so, on his account, the violence stops. The texts of the persecutors carry four marks that let a later reader see through them. So far those marks have been read only where the truth has to be inferred. Whether they give the right verdict where the truth is known from outside is the next thing to check.

Part 7: Where the Verdict Can Be Checked

The four marks work like an instrument, and an instrument has to be checked on cases where history kept the answer. The claim is that it passes. Where the truth is known from outside the persecutors' own account, the four marks gave the right verdict. And those same cases show what turns a disaster into a killing. The size of the disaster matters less than three other things: fear inside a group, an accusation already moving, and a people already marked.

The first case is Strasbourg in the plague years, where a chronicler wrote down what happened. The obvious guess is the order everyone assumes. The plague arrives, people die in terror, and the terrified turn on the Jews. The chronicle gives a different order. Early in February 1349, at a regional meeting, "the Bishop and the lords and the Imperial Cities agreed to do away with the Jews". Strasbourg's own deputies were asked what they meant to do with theirs. They answered that they "knew no evil of them".101 Within days, the town council that wanted to save the Jews was thrown out, and the new council gave in to the mob.102 On the fourteenth of February the Jews were burned on a wooden platform in their own cemetery, "about two thousand people of them". Those who agreed to be baptised were spared.103

101quotation
At a regional meeting in early February 1349, the Bishop and the lords and the Imperial Cities agreed to do away with the Jews, and Strasbourg's own deputies, asked what they meant to do with theirs, said they knew no evil of them.
Jacob Twinger von Königshofen, chronicle, trans. in Jacob R. Marcus, The Jew in the Medieval World: A Sourcebook, 315-1791 (New York: JPS, 1938), 43-48; Internet Jewish History Sourcebook, "The Black Death and the Jews, 1348-1349 CE"

Then comes the line that settles the test. The plague, the chronicler writes, "came to Strasbourg in the summer" of that year, and about sixteen thousand people died.104 The supposed poisoners had been dead for months when the disease arrived. So the city's own record shows that the charge could not be true, and no knowledge of germs is needed to see it. The chronicler even names the motive he believed lay underneath. "The money was indeed the thing that killed the Jews". The debts owed to them were cancelled, and the council shared out their cash among the working men.105 The doubters did not lose the argument so much as lose their seats.

104quotation

Strasbourg is the unusual case, and that needs saying. Most massacres came while the plague was already raging. The few towns that struck before it arrived all acted after a forced confession at a Swiss castle sent the poison rumour down the Rhine.106 This fits the earlier finding instead of overturning it. Beyond a point the worst-hit towns persecuted less, and as many as ten towns may have persecuted before anyone had died.107 Both results point the same way: the burnings tracked old hatred, rumour and money more closely than they tracked the body count.

106quotation
Most Black Death massacres came while the plague was already raging, but in some places the news of its approach alone was enough to inflame people; a minority of towns persecuted before any outbreak, after the Chillon confession sent the well-poisoning rumour down the Rhine.

The accusation was older than the plague, and it had already been used on someone else. In 1321, a generation before the great plague, a rumour ran through Aquitaine. The springs and wells, it said, had been poisoned by the lepers. Many lepers confessed when they were put to the fire, and were burned.108 Then, the chronicler reports, a leper confessed that a rich Jew had paid him to corrupt the others. And the Jews were burned in their turn.109 The crime stayed the same while the target moved. A charge of poisoning the common water works like a form kept in a drawer, and the moment fills in the name.

108quotation

Now a harder test, aimed at the loosest wording in the theory. If crisis raises the odds of a victim, catastrophe itself ought to breed scapegoats: a flood, a fire, a town flattened by a storm. A disaster sociologist who gathered the field studies of sudden disasters in 1950s America found close to the opposite. In his words, "Not a single minority group has been subjected to scapegoating activity" in any of the peacetime disasters studied. Solidarity among the stricken rose sharply.110 People did assign blame, but to whoever was responsible for fixing things and preventing a repeat. And only a minority felt any resentment even toward them.111 He drew his limits himself. The evidence came from peacetime disasters that struck once and passed, and he offered it as a starting idea, not a law.112 What survives is enough for the argument: a disaster, on its own, does not pick a victim. So the first mark needs a word added: not a disaster alone, but fear inside a group.

110quotation
A disaster sociologist who gathered the field studies of sudden peacetime disasters in 1950s America found that not a single minority group was subjected to scapegoating in any of them, and that solidarity among the stricken rose sharply.
Charles E. Fritz, Disasters and Mental Health: Therapeutic Principles Drawn from Disaster Studies (written 1961; published 1996, University of Delaware Disaster Research Center, Historical and Comparative Disaster Series #10)
pp. 45-46, section "Reduction or Prevention of Self-Aggressive and Anti-Social Behavior"
Read the passage
Charles E. Fritz, Disasters and Mental Health (1961/1996)
p. 10 (Preface)
Read the passage
How it holds
This is the guess-then-result beat: the obvious guess that any disaster breeds a scapegoat is tested and found close to the opposite in this sample.
What it does not say
"Studied in recent years" means American peacetime field studies of the 1950s; Fritz limits his most systematic findings to "the single impact type of peacetime disaster" and calls the framework "ideal-typical" and "heuristic". Kanto 1923 is outside his sample and does not contradict a law; Fritz does not cite Girard and never considered 1923 Tokyo.

On the first of September 1923, an earthquake struck the region around Tokyo. The shaking and the fires after it killed more than a hundred thousand people.113 On the first day, the police logged a rumour that Koreans were setting the fires. From the second day the rumour added poison, thrown by Koreans into the wells.114 Korea was then ruled by Japan, and a government report written long afterwards names the background. Japan feared the resistance to its colonial rule, and there was incomprehension and ethnic discrimination as well.115 From the second night, vigilante groups formed in nearly every neighbourhood, most of them on their own. They set up checkpoints, questioned passers-by, and beat Koreans and anyone else they found suspicious.116

Run the four marks. The crisis was total, a region shaken down and burning, and a city in fear. The crime was the one from Aquitaine and the Rhine, poisoned wells, with fire added. The victims carried the oldest sign, a minority the majority already feared. And the violence was real. This time there is an outside record, which the plague poem never had. Within a week, the police chief told the neighbourhood youth groups that most of the rumours had proved entirely groundless. That November, the Justice Ministry reported that it could find no trace of the planned uprising, arson and poisoning the rumours described.120 On the question the four marks answer, that record agrees with them. The crime was invented, and the victims were chosen for their signs.

The Justice Ministry's report still listed a few individual murders attributed to Koreans with unknown suspects, and denied only the organised plot; the same government also decided to hide the Ōshima killings of Chinese, so the "outside record" is a mix of refutation and concealment.

The checkpoints also show the third mark working by itself, with nobody steering it. A sign is not evidence, and at a roadside in the dark it cannot tell a stranger from a neighbour. The same report records that people who spoke poorly, or with a strong dialect, were questioned or beaten. By the Justice Ministry's rough count, fifty-eight Japanese were killed after being taken for Korean by their looks, their manner or their speech.121 The report adds that the very act of stopping people in the dark fed the panic, for the ones stopping and the ones stopped alike. One writer was stopped by his own neighbours, who told him that even among acquaintances, today was different.122 In the plague poem, a victim chosen by signs rather than by evidence was something a reader had to infer. Here it is on record, and the signs caught members of the crowd's own people.

None of this is a charge against one nation. The oldest checkpoint in any written story is in the Bible. In the book of Judges, the men of Gilead hold the crossings of the Jordan against the fleeing Ephraimites. Each man who reaches the river is asked whether he is an Ephraimite. If he says no, there is a second test.123 "Say now Shibboleth: and he said Sibboleth: for he could not frame to pronounce it right". Then they took him and killed him at the crossing.124 The verses never say why the test was needed, so what follows is a reading. They had to ask, which means they could not tell by looking. The outsider who watches two factions fight sees two groups that are nearly identical. A checkpoint is what a crowd builds when its enemy looks exactly like itself, and the mark is made to tell the doubles apart. The report on 1923 draws a broader lesson. Every society holds groups felt to be different, and any of them can become a target.125

The last case asks how long a mark lasts. In the plague years, the study of plague towns found that a history of hatred made persecution more likely.126 Two economists carried that finding six centuries forward. They compared German towns that burned their Jews in the plague years with nearby towns that had no pogrom. Six hundred years of wars, migrations and new rulers did not wash the difference out.127 In the 1920s, attacks on Jews were about six times as likely in the towns that had once burned their Jews. In 1928, the Nazi vote there ran more than a third higher. And the authors state their own limit: most of the pogrom towns saw no attacks at all in the 1920s.128 So the mark is a raised chance, not a fate. The mark crossed the centuries the way any culture lasts, by people taking on what those around them already hold. It measures a memory, and nothing in it measures the calm a killing is said to bring.

126fact
In the Black Death, the study of plague towns found that a town's prior history of hatred toward Jews predicted a persecution, where the death toll did not.
Remi Jedwab, Noel D. Johnson and Mark Koyama, "Negative Shocks and Mass Persecutions: Evidence from the Black Death", MPRA Paper 77720 (March 2017); published as Journal of Economic Growth 24(4), 2019, 345-395 (bibliographic record only, not read)
abstract; pp. 2, 13-14 (sample and baseline), 17 (IV), 24-29 (mechanisms)
Read the passage
Remi Jedwab, Noel D. Johnson and Mark Koyama, "Negative Shocks and Mass Persecutions: Evidence from the Black Death", MPRA Paper 77720 (March 2017); published as Journal of Economic Growth 24(4), 2019, 345-395 (bibliographic record only, not read)
abstract
Read the passage
How it holds
Recalls the Part 6 finding (p6-c18) in the study's own terms.
What it does not say
The paper reports that a history of antisemitism made persecution more likely and that higher mortality made it less likely; it does not rank its predictors, so 'best' is the script's comparison with the death toll. Its past-persecution measure counts recent violence (1340-1346); a 50-year window shows no effect. The paper's 'persecution' covers massacres and expulsions, not burnings alone.

Two economists compared German towns that burned their Jews in the plague years with nearby towns that had no pogrom, and found the difference survived six hundred years of wars, migrations and new rulers.

The towns compared are matched pairs across six centuries of German history.
What it does not say
What the authors did is compare towns WITH a 1349 pogrom against nearby towns WITHOUT one, matched by geography (10-25 km apart), on 1920s-30s outcomes.

In the 1920s, attacks on Jews were about six times as likely in towns that had once burned their Jews, and in 1928 the Nazi vote there ran more than a third higher; the authors state that most of the pogrom towns saw no attacks at all in the 1920s.
Nico Voigtländer and Hans-Joachim Voth, "Persecution Perpetuated: The Medieval Origins of Anti-Semitic Violence in Nazi Germany", NBER Working Paper 17113 (2011)

printed p. 17
Read the passage
How it holds
The body wording resolves an inconsistency between the paper's own introduction and its detailed result.
What it does not say
The paper's intro states "1.5 times higher" for the 1928 Nazi vote, but the body (p. 18) gives "approximately 1.5% higher" on an average of about 3.5%, i.e. "more than a third"; the script correctly uses the body's wording, not the intro's "1.5 times". The NSDAP's 1928 nationwide vote share was only 2.6%, so this is a small party's vote.

So the loose claim that crisis raises the odds of a victim can be sharpened. In these cases the size of the disaster was not what decided it. Three things were present together. One, a crisis of fear inside the group, whether or not the dying had begun. Two, an accusation already in circulation, as it was in 1321, ready to be repeated. Three, a group already marked, often for centuries, and set apart by signs because it is otherwise so like the crowd. Where all three met and an outside record survives, in Strasbourg and around Tokyo, the four marks gave the verdict that record confirmed. In both, those in power joined in, and in Strasbourg money was at stake, so the copying had help. Both cases were chosen because history already knew the answer, so they show the marks can point right, not how often they point wrong. The shape of a persecution text can point to an invented crime and an innocent victim. It says nothing about a founding murder at the start of religion. That stays a hypothesis, as before.

So the method has been checked where it can be checked. A disaster on its own picked no one, while fear, a ready accusation and an old mark did, with power and money helping. The mark, applied at a roadside, killed members of the crowd's own people. And later readers can see through the persecutors' own texts, with new reason to trust what they see. But the persecutors do not see through them. From inside, the crowd has just watched a disaster end the moment one person was removed. What it concludes about that person, and what it does with that conclusion, is the next step.

Part 8: The Victim Becomes a God

A crowd that has just killed its victim and found itself at peace draws two conclusions about him, and they contradict each other. He caused the disaster, because his removal ended it. And he caused the peace, because the peace came from him. A being who can bring both plague and healing is more than a man. Girard's claim is that this is where the sacred begins: in a victim blamed for the crisis and then credited with the cure. And myths, on his reading, are the stories crowds told about such events, from the side that did the killing.

Begin with the crowd's point of view, because it is the only one the crowd has. For weeks there was chaos. Neighbours fought neighbours, the harvest failed, people died. Then the whole town turned on one person, and the moment he was gone, the fighting stopped. Nobody in that town knows that their own unanimity did it. The mechanism works only because they do not see it. What they saw was simpler: this one person's presence brought ruin, and his removal brought peace. Girard put the double conclusion in one sentence. "The victim is held responsible for the renewed calm in the community and for the disorder that preceded this return".129 The same person is the poison and the remedy.

129quotation
Girard put the double conclusion in one sentence: "The victim is held responsible for the renewed calm in the community and for the disorder that preceded this return."
Rene Girard with J.-M. Oughourlian and G. Lefort, Things Hidden Since the Foundation of the World, trans. Stephen Bann and Michael Metteer (Stanford UP, 1987), Book I, ch. 1 "The Victimage Mechanism as the Basis of Religion"
Book I, ch. 1, p. 27

What kind of being has that power? Not an ordinary criminal, who causes harm but cannot cure it. Not an ordinary benefactor, who helps but never threatens. Only something that stands outside the ordinary order and can make it or unmake it at will. That is what the oldest gods were: dangerous, unpredictable, needing to be kept at a distance and placated, and also the source of every good thing. Girard noticed how often the myths end with this turn. They "conclude with the triumphal return of the sufferer, thereby revealed as a divinity".130 The one who was expelled comes back as the one who must be honoured.

130quotation
Girard noticed that such myths "conclude with the triumphal return of the sufferer, thereby revealed as a divinity."
Rene Girard, "Are the Gospels Mythical?", First Things 62 (April 1996), 27-31

Now the case, read the way the plague poem was read. The play is the most famous in the Greek theatre. It opens on a city in disaster. "The flaming god, the malign plague, hath swooped on us".131 Crops fail, cattle die, women give birth to dead children. The oracle is consulted, and it says the city must drive out "a defiling thing", by banishing a man or by bloodshed.132 That is the first mark: a crisis that strikes everyone alike, and a city afraid. Girard opens his own reading with the same observation. "The plague is ravaging Thebes: here we have the first stereotype of persecution".133

The second mark is the crime. The seer accuses the king. "Thou art the accursed defiler of this land", he tells him.134 And the crimes come out one by one. Oedipus has killed his own father, and he is living, as the seer puts it, "in unguessed shame with thy nearest kin".135 Parricide and incest. The two crimes that dissolve the most basic differences of all, between father and son, between mother and wife. They are the same kind of crime the plague poem charged against the Jews: a crime big enough to explain why everything has collapsed.

The third mark is the victim's signs, and Oedipus carries almost all of them. He arrived in the city as a stranger. He is the king, at the very top, which is the other extreme of being set apart. And he is lame. In the play an old servant reminds him that as a baby he was found with his ankles pinned together.136 Girard's phrase for Oedipus is that his traits make him "a veritable conglomerate of victim's signs".137 The fourth mark is the expulsion. Oedipus blinds himself and begs, "Cast me out of this land with all speed".138 The play does not stage the cure. But the oracle has promised one, and the whole drama moves toward a single man's removal as the city's salvation.

Put the play beside the plague poem and all four marks line up. When we read Machaut, we conclude without hesitation that the Jews were innocent and the crowd was wrong. Girard's challenge is simply to read Sophocles the same way. And the story did not end with the expulsion. In a later play by the same poet, the old, blind, exiled Oedipus comes to die near Athens. His grave is promised as a protection to the city that receives him.139 The defiler of one city becomes the guardian of another. That is the double conclusion, carried all the way through: the man blamed for the plague becomes a source of blessing.

139fact
In a later play, the old, blind, exiled Oedipus comes to die near Athens, and his grave is promised as a protection to the city that receives him.
Sophocles, Oedipus at Colonus, trans. R. C. Jebb, in The Tragedies of Sophocles (Cambridge UP, 1917)
about lines 576-580
Read the passage
Sophocles, Oedipus at Colonus, trans. R. C. Jebb, in The Tragedies of Sophocles (Cambridge UP, 1917)
about lines 1520-1525
Read the passage
How it holds
Oedipus offers his body to Athens as a gift whose worth shows after his burial, and tells Theseus the hidden grave will defend the city better than shields.
What it does not say
The protection is promised to Athens through Theseus, who alone is to know where the grave is; the play does not say the city killed Oedipus.

Girard had a word for this. He calls it the sacred, and he means by that word something more precise than holiness. The sacred is the power the crowd attributes to its victim, because it cannot see its own violence. It is feared because the victim was blamed for the crisis. It is worshipped because the victim was credited with the peace. Both feelings attach to the same being, and that doubleness is the mark of the sacred in the oldest religions.

And here is what this does to myth. On the common view, myths are fanciful stories about gods and heroes, invented to explain the world or to entertain. On Girard's view they are something darker and more specific: records of real persecutions, told by the persecutors. "The conjunction of the guilty victim and the reconciled community is too frequent to be fortuitous", he wrote.140 Again and again, the myth's plot is a crisis, a guilty figure, his removal and the community's peace. He called myth "the unanimous self-deception" of a crowd that cannot see what it did.141 The common view keeps something true: myths do contain invention, and do explain things. What it misses, on this reading, is that the inventions have a shape, and the shape is a crowd's account of a killing.

140quotation
"The conjunction of the guilty victim and the reconciled community is too frequent to be fortuitous."
Rene Girard, "Are the Gospels Mythical?", First Things 62 (April 1996), 27-31
web text, no page numbers

What it does not say

Now the objection, and it is the most serious one the whole theory faces. A founding murder behind every myth cannot be dug up. No one was there, no records exist, and the theory itself says the records were written to hide what happened. One scholar, introducing a published debate between Girard and his critics, put the problem in five words: "The data appear not to be available".142 A theory that predicts its own evidence will have been concealed is very hard to test. And a reviewer in a major literary journal added the plain empirical point, that "few texts fit the theory very neatly".143 There is a third point from the historians of Greek religion. In the Greek rites that are actually recorded, the human scapegoat was driven out and, as one put it, "certainly not killed".144 The killings appear in the myths, not in the rites.

142quotation

Each of these needs a straight answer. The first is conceded in full. The founding murder is a hypothesis. Nobody can date it, locate it or prove it happened, and nobody can honestly pretend otherwise. What can be checked is the pattern of marks, which is visible in the texts themselves. Any reader can test it against any myth they like. The hypothesis earns its keep by explaining why that pattern is there, the way a good theory of an unseen cause explains the effects it leaves. The second point is partly conceded. Many myths do not fit, and the theory's reach is narrower than Girard sometimes claimed. But the ones that do fit include some of the most important the ancient world produced. Girard argued that other explanations fit those marks less well.145 The third point should be conceded too. The rites show expulsion, not killing, and reading them as a softened killing assumes the very killing the record lacks. What the rites do show is a group removing one of its own to set itself right, and that much fits the shape.

145fact
Girard argues that only a real persecution behind the myths explains why the same themes recur in the same pattern.
René Girard, The Scapegoat, trans. Yvonne Freccero (Baltimore: Johns Hopkins UP, 1986)
ch. 3, "What Is a Myth?", p. 28
print copy
How it holds
Girard claims that without a real persecution at the origin the recurrence of the same pattern cannot be explained.
What it does not say
Girard argues this; he does not test rival theories one by one here. Read in a digital copy of this printed edition; it is not linked because the free copies online are unauthorised uploads of an in-copyright book. The page number is the printed page, checked in a scan of this edition. A free borrowable scan of the same edition (free account) is at archive.org/details/scapegoat0000gira/page/28.

So the account of the sacred is complete. The crowd that finds peace after killing its victim blames him for the crisis and credits him with the peace. That double conclusion makes him sacred, feared and worshipped at once. Myths, read with the four marks in mind, look like the crowd's account of such events, with the guilt placed on the victim. The founding murder itself remains a hypothesis that explains a pattern, not an event anyone can show. But many myths show no killing at all. That could be a hole in the theory, or it could be something the theory predicts, and can be caught out on.

Part 9: How a Myth Hides Its Murder

The hardest objection to the theory was met with a promise: any reader can test it against any myth they like. That promise can be kept, and the test has teeth. The four marks gave the right verdict in the cases where history could check them, but no court record stands beside a myth. So the test has to come from inside the stories. The claim is this. A myth that hides a killing leaves traces of a particular kind, and the hiding runs in one direction. Where those traces are in the text, a hidden-murder reading earns trust. Where the reader has to bring the violence from outside, the reading fails, and some readings do fail.

If the crowd hid the killing, then a myth with no killing fits the theory too, as a cover-up that worked. Then no myth could ever count against it. The way out is to ask what hiding would actually look like. A myth is not written once. It is retold for centuries, by people further and further from whatever happened. Suppose those people grow uneasy about violence among their gods. Then each retelling should soften the story a little, and the softening should run one way. A killing by everyone becomes a killing by one. A murder becomes an accident, or a game. A victim dragged to his death becomes a victim who volunteers. And at the last step, the story stops being told at all. Softening by many hands over many generations is never tidy, so it should leave a trace. The violence should survive somewhere in the text, moved off centre but still visible. That is a prediction, and a given myth can fail it, by showing no trace at all.

The first thing to check is whether anyone ever softened a myth on purpose. That needs no guess, because a plan for it survives in writing. In the Republic, Plato has his speaker take up the poets' story of the god Cronus, and how he "retaliated" on his own father. He calls it "that greatest of all lies".146 Then he turns to how Cronus struck back at his own father, and suffered in turn at his son's hands. Even if those stories were true, he says, they "ought certainly not to be lightly told to young and thoughtless persons".147 His first choice: "if possible, they had better be buried in silence".148 Failing that, a few may hear them, after first sacrificing "some huge and unprocurable victim".149 In his ideal city they are "stories not to be repeated".150

146quotation
In the Republic, Plato's speaker (Socrates) takes up the poets' story of how Cronus retaliated on his own father, Uranus, and calls it the greatest of all lies.
Plato, The Republic, trans. Benjamin Jowett, Book II
Stephanus c. 377e (Wikisource page prints no Stephanus numbers)
Read the passage
Plato, The Republic, trans. Benjamin Jowett, Book II
Stephanus c. 377e-378a
Read the passage
How it holds
Opens the chapter's first test case: a culture's own heir proposing to edit violence out of its myths.
What it does not say
Plato treats these stories as false and harmful, not as a witness to a real killing he believes happened.

This is the last step of the softening the chapter looks for, written down as policy by the most famous philosopher of his culture.

It matters what this does and does not show. Plato thinks these stories are false and harmful to the young, so he is no witness to some hidden killing. What he shows is the motive and the method. The most famous philosopher of his culture proposes, on the page, to take violence among the gods out of what the young will hear. And he would bury it in silence if he could. That is the last step of the softening, written down as policy. So the process the theory needs is more than a guess about some vanished crowd. An heir uneasy with a myth's violence proposed to edit it, and left his reasons on record. Whether later tellings really softened in one direction is the theory's prediction, not something shown here.

Now the trace. Anyone would expect a well-hidden killing to leave nothing behind, and if that were so, the test would be hopeless. An old northern story about the death of the god Baldr shows something different. All things, the story says, swore an oath not to harm Baldr: fire and water, iron and stone, sickness, beasts, birds and poison.151 So the gods made a game of it. At their assembly Baldr would stand up, and all the others would "some shoot at him, some hew at him, some beat him with stones". Nothing hurt him, and the gods thought that "a very worshipful thing".152

One plant had not sworn. The mistletoe had been thought too young to ask. At the edge of the game stood a blind god, who "stood outside the ring of men, because he was blind". Loki put the mistletoe in his hand and guided his aim.153 The shaft flew through Baldr, and he fell dead to the earth. Words failed all the gods, the story says, and their hands failed them too. And then the line that matters most: "none might take vengeance, so great a sanctuary was in that place".154

Run the moves against it. Every god striking at one god is still there, almost word for word, turned into a game. The gods even stand in a ring around him, in the text's own image. The killing by everyone has become a killing by one, and that one is blind, with his aim guided by another. The guilt has a single home, in Loki, the one figure the story can blame. And the place is holy, so nobody may avenge the death there; the story takes its vengeance later, all of it on Loki. Recall the line about the crowd's peace: a killing by everyone is avenged by no one. Here a myth says the same thing about its own killing, and gives the reason as the holiness of the place. The reader supplies none of the violence. The ring, the blows and the single target are all in the text, softened into play.

The third move, the volunteer, needs a second myth, and it comes from Mexico. A Spanish friar wrote it down in his history of the things of New Spain. Before there was day in the world, the gods gathered and asked who would take on the charge of lighting it. A proud god spoke up and said he would.155 The gods asked who would be the other one. Then they themselves named a second, a god of no account, covered in sores, who never spoke and only listened. He answered that he received what they had commanded as a favour, and so be it.156

Before the fire, the proud volunteer tried four times, and never dared to throw himself in. Then the gods called on the sore-covered god. He closed his eyes, rushed forward, and threw himself into the fire. Only when the proud god saw him burning did he leap in after him.157 Sun and moon rose, but they stood still in one place. The gods asked how they could live like that, and decided: let us all die. The wind took on the task of killing all the gods, and killed them.158 One god refused. He begged not to die, ran, and hid in the water as a fish. They took him from there and killed him. Even then, the sun moved on its way only when the wind began to blow hard.159

157fact
Before the fire, the proud volunteer tried four times and never dared to throw himself in; the gods then called on the sore-covered god, who closed his eyes, rushed forward and threw himself into the fire, and only then did the proud god leap in after him.

Read with the moves in hand, the Mexican story shows the softened shapes and keeps traces, though only one version of it survives. The marked god is chosen by the group, and the choice then turns into his own consent. His sores set him apart, and they can be read as a victim's sign, the third of the four marks. The friar himself says only that he was covered in sores. The eager volunteer is the one who cannot go through with it. And at the end, the killing by all is still there in the text. The gods all die by their own decision, and the one who runs is caught and killed anyway.

Now the case where the method breaks. In a Greek story, the infant king of the gods is born in a cave and hidden from his father. The father is Cronus, the same god whose story Plato wanted buried. Armed young men stand round the baby. In one old handbook's words, they "guarded the babe in the cave". They were "clashing their spears on their shields in order that Cronus might not hear the child's voice".160 A ring of armed men around a helpless child has the outline of a crowd and its victim. Girard read it that way, as a scene whose play-acting is "reminiscent of collective murder". To his credit, he called his own reading "speculative, even more than in the case of Baldr".161

160quotation
In a Greek story, the infant king of the gods (Zeus) is born and hidden from his father Cronus in a cave, and armed young men guard the baby there, clashing their spears on their shields so that Cronus will not hear the child's voice.
Apollodorus, The Library, trans. Sir James George Frazer (Loeb, 1921)
1.1.6
Read the passage

Apply the test. The violence would have to be in the text, moved off centre but still visible, and it is nowhere. Nothing strikes the child, and no one dies. The noise has a reason given in the story itself, and the reason is to protect him. To find a murder here, the reader has to carry it in from outside and lay it over a scene of care.162 The method, pushed this far, turns into what its critics said it was. It becomes a habit of seeing a murder in anything shaped like a crowd and a victim. The reviewer who said that few texts fit the theory very neatly is right about this one. The case does not count, and here Girard's reading goes too far, as his own word for it half admits.

162fact
Applying the test to the Curetes scene, nothing strikes the child and no one dies; the noise is given a protective reason in the story itself, so a reader has to carry the violence in from outside to find a murder there.
Apollodorus, The Library, trans. Sir James George Frazer (Loeb, 1921)
1.1.7
Read the passage
How it holds
This is the case the chapter uses to show the method can fail, and does.
What it does not say
The text gives the noise a protective purpose and the Curetes are guards; nothing in it threatens or harms the child.

That gives a rule anyone can carry to any myth. A reading that finds a hidden murder earns trust only where the text itself keeps the violence and its single target, displaced but visible. The gods' game of striking Baldr passes. The account from Mexico passes in part, on the volunteer and on the one god hunted down, though no blows from all fall on one target. Where the reader must bring the violence, the reading could never fail, so it proves nothing, and nothing here leans on it. The test is the same each time. Look for a group, one target, blows from all, a death, and a reason given why nobody pays for it. Where those survive under a game, a volunteer or a holy place, the softening has left its trace. Where none of them survive, nothing is hidden there to find.

That changes the standing of the objection without making it go away. A myth that fails this test does not count against the theory; it only falls outside it. And all three myths here are ones Girard himself chose, so this shows the test can fail, not how much of mythology the theory holds.163 It can be tested one myth at a time, and it fails some of them. That is what it means, in practice, that many myths do not fit. The founding murder itself is still a hypothesis, and no myth digs it up. What has changed is that "the myth hides it" now predicts something specific, and can be caught out. Whatever the myths hide or keep, a peace bought with a killing still has to be held on to. That is what the oldest institutions of religion were for.

163fact
All three myths used to test the method in this chapter (Baldr, the Mexican fire myth, and the Curetes) are myths Girard himself discussed, in The Scapegoat's chapters on Teotihuacan and on Baldr and the Curetes.
René Girard, The Scapegoat, trans. Yvonne Freccero (Johns Hopkins UP, 1986), table of contents
ch. 5 "Teotihuacan" (begins p. 57); ch. 6 "Ases, Curetes, and Titans" (pp. 66-75)
print copy
How it holds
Confirms, from the book's own table of contents, that Girard treats Teotihuacan, Baldr and the Curetes together as instances of the same pattern.
What it does not say
The three myths are Girard's own choice of cases, so they cannot show how much of mythology fits the theory. Part 10: Rules, Rites and the Substitute

The calm after a killing does not last on its own. The rivalries that caused the crisis start again the next morning, because people go on copying each other's desires. Girard's claim is that the oldest religions are the means by which groups kept that peace going. Rules forbid the copying that led to the crisis. Rites act out the crisis and the killing again, under control, so their calm can be had again. And sacrifice puts a substitute in the victim's place, so that the cure can be repeated without a new lynching every time.

The question is simple: why would anyone kill an animal on an altar. The usual answer is that it was a gift, a payment to a god, food offered in exchange for rain or victory. The god is hungry or angry, and the offering buys him off. That is the view most of us learned, and it is not foolish. Many peoples did speak of their offerings as gifts. Now set beside it the word the Athenians themselves used. An old dictionary of Athenian terms records that at a spring festival they "led out" two men. One was for the men of the city and one for the women. The men were called pharmakoi, and the dictionary says they were led out as purifications of the city. Not gifts. Purifications.164 Something was being cleaned out of Athens, and two human beings were how it was done.

164quotation
An old dictionary of Athenian terms records that at a festival they "led out" two men, one for the men of the city and one for the women, called pharmakoi, as purifications of the city.
Valerius Harpocration, Lexicon in decem oratores Atticos, ed. W. Dindorf (Oxford, 1853), s.v. pharmakos; citing Lysias, Against Andocides (Lys. 6), and Istros, Epiphanies of Apollo book 1
letter phi, entry pharmakos
Read the passage
How it holds
Grounds the Athenian pharmakos rite as a purification rather than a gift, which is the hinge of this part.
What it does not say
The rite is the Thargelia, which the note does not describe as a "spring" festival (it falls near early summer); the script's "spring festival" is unverified by this source and should be checked. Harpocration's verb is "led out" (exegon), not "killed"; whether historical Athenian pharmakoi were killed or only expelled is disputed; Girard himself asserts the men were killed, which the next row (on Girard's "slaughtered" claim) sets against this testimony.

That word changes the question. A gift is about the god. A purification is about the community: something has gone wrong inside the group, and someone is removed to set it right. That is the shape of the scapegoat's peace, a group that turns on one of its own and is calmer afterwards. The Athenian rite looks like that event, made regular and held on a fixed day of the year. Girard wrote that these men were slaughtered at the festival, but the oldest notice says only that they were led out. An expulsion fits the shape, though it is not evidence of a killing behind it.165

165fact
Girard wrote that these men were slaughtered at the festival, but the oldest notice says only that they were led out; an expulsion fits the shape, though it is not evidence of a killing behind it.
Rene Girard, Violence and the Sacred, trans. Patrick Gregory (Johns Hopkins UP, 1977), ch. 1, repr. as ch. 6 "Sacrifice as Sacral Violence and Substitution" in The Girard Reader (Crossroad, 1996)
Girard Reader ch. 6, "Sacrifice as Sacral Violence and Substitution", p. 79

Death is attested only for the mythical Pharmakos (said to be stoned) and in the late Byzantine Tzetzes (not read); whether historical Athenian pharmakoi were killed is disputed among classicists. There is a second scene of the same shape, and its name is where the English word comes from. In the Hebrew law for the Day of Atonement, the priest lays his hands on a goat's head. He confesses over it the sins of the people. Then the goat is driven out. "And the goat shall bear upon him all their iniquities unto a land not inhabited".166 A whole people's wrongdoing is gathered onto one creature, and the creature is sent away. The scapegoat in our language is that goat.

166quotation
In the Day of Atonement rite, after the priest confesses the people's sins over a goat, it is driven out: "And the goat shall bear upon him all their iniquities unto a land not inhabited."
Leviticus 16:21-22, King James Version
Lev 16:21-22
Read the passage
How it holds
Grounds the Leviticus rite that the English word "scapegoat" comes from.
What it does not say
This is the biblical ritual law itself, not a report of any historical practice being carried out; it licenses the description of the rite's text, not a claim that this exact ceremony is attested outside scripture.

Now Girard's full account, in three pieces, each with its job. The first piece is the rule. If the crisis came from rivalry spreading, the most useful thing a group can do is forbid the behaviour that spreads it. Girard put it carefully: "Religious prohibitions make a good deal of sense when interpreted as efforts to prevent mimetic rivalry from spreading throughout human communities".167 Rules about who may touch what, who may marry whom, who may stand where and wear what, all keep people from reaching for the same things. Recall the fear of twins. A rule that keeps doubles apart is a rule that stops the fire before it starts.

167quotation
Girard: "Religious prohibitions make a good deal of sense when interpreted as efforts to prevent mimetic rivalry from spreading throughout human communities."
Rene Girard, "Mimesis and Violence", in The Girard Reader, ed. James G. Williams (Crossroad, 1996), ch. 1
Girard Reader ch. 1, "Mimesis and Violence", p. 10
print copy
How it holds
Direct quotation, exact match.
What it does not say
Read in a digital copy of this printed edition; it is not linked because the free copies online are unauthorised uploads of an in-copyright book. The page number is the printed page of the Crossroad edition, read from the page markers of a digital copy and checked against the book's own contents page. A free borrowable scan of the same edition (free account) is at archive.org/details/girardreader0000gira/page/10.

One rule says it outright, and it is among the most famous rules ever written down. In the Ten Commandments, the ones near the end forbid acts. "Thou shalt not kill". "Thou shalt not steal". "Thou shalt not bear false witness against thy neighbour".168 Then the last commandment forbids something that is not an act at all. It begins, "Thou shalt not covet thy neighbour's house, thou shalt not covet thy neighbour's wife". It goes on through his servants, his ox and his ass. Then the list gives up naming things, and ends, "nor any thing that is thy neighbour's".169 Listen to what stays the same through that list. The objects keep changing, a house, a wife, an animal, and finally any object at all. What never changes is the neighbour. The text does not say why, but the shape is hard to miss. A list that forbids killing ends by forbidding a wanting. And it names where that wanting points: at the person next door. That is where a law against rivalry would have to look, because the near model is the dangerous one. Girard read the commandment the same way: "it forbids a desire".170

168quotation
The commandments just before the last one forbid acts: killing, stealing, bearing false witness against one's neighbour.
Exodus 20, King James Version

What it does not say
False witness is speech, not a bodily act; "acts" is fair in the sense of things done, not things wanted. The script omits Ex 20:14 (adultery), which is also in this group but not quoted.

The second piece is the rite. Here Girard noticed something odd. Many rites do not begin in solemnity. They begin in disorder. "Many rituals begin with a mimetic free-for-all during which hierarchies disintegrate".171 That is a free-for-all of copying, everyone against everyone. Then a killing ends the disorder, in what he calls "the happy solution of ritual sacrifice with the help of a substitute victim".172 The rite replays the crisis on purpose, and then replays the ending.

171quotation
Girard: "Many rituals begin with a mimetic free-for-all during which hierarchies disintegrate."

A group stages its own worst moment for a reason. The rite is a memory of how the crisis ended, kept in the only form that people who do not understand the mechanism can keep it. They do not know that a crowd's unanimity calmed them. They know that disorder came, a victim was struck, and peace followed. So they do it again: disorder, victim, peace. Girard saw that the rule and the rite seem opposed, one forbidding disorder and the other staging it. Yet he said they serve one end: "The purpose of both is to spare the community another mimetic perturbation".173

173quotation
Girard says prohibition and rite serve one end: "The purpose of both is to spare the community another mimetic perturbation."
Rene Girard, "Mimesis and Violence", in The Girard Reader, ed. James G. Williams (Crossroad, 1996), ch. 1
Girard Reader ch. 1, "Mimesis and Violence", p. 13

A free borrowable scan of the same edition (free account) is at archive.org/details/girardreader0000gira/page/13.

The third piece is the substitute. The rite cannot kill a member of the group every year, or it would itself become the violence it was meant to prevent. So the victim is replaced. On Girard's account the substitutes ran from captives and outsiders down to animals.174 Girard describes the sacrificial victim as one that is "a substitute for all the members of the community, offered up by the members themselves".175 The whole group's violence is gathered onto one body that nobody will avenge. He calls sacrifice "primarily an act of violence without risk of vengeance".176 The Norse myth said something close in its own words, over Baldr's body, in a holy place where none might take vengeance.177 That phrase joins sacrifice back to the feud. A feud is violence that will always be answered. A sacrifice is violence that nobody answers, because the victim belongs to no family that will strike back. On his account, the animal on the altar is the last of these stand-ins for the one the crowd turned on.

174fact
Girard describes sacrificial victims ranging from prisoners of war and slaves to animals.

p. 104
Read the passage
How it holds
Callback to the Baldr myth examined at length in Part 9; the phrase joins Girard's account of sacrifice back to the logic of the feud.

The three pieces share one frame. A group has once found peace by uniting against a victim. It forbids what led to the crisis. It repeats the ending, under control, with a victim nobody will avenge. And it calls the whole arrangement holy, because the peace it gives seems to come from outside. The Athenian rite, the goat in the wilderness and the animal on the altar are three versions of that frame. Each is the scapegoat's peace, made into a practice a group can keep.

The court, whose punishment nobody avenges, belongs in this line too. Girard saw it as the last heir of this whole line. He called our legal system the institution "which serves to deflect the menace of vengeance".178 And he observed that "sacrifice has languished in societies with a firmly established judicial system".179 Where courts are strong, the altar fades, and Girard's explanation is that the court does the altar's work more openly. The difference is that the court claims to strike the guilty, while the altar never asked.

178quotation
Girard called our legal system the institution "which serves to deflect the menace of vengeance."
Rene Girard, Violence and the Sacred, trans. Patrick Gregory (Johns Hopkins UP, 1977), ch. 1, repr. as ch. 6 in The Girard Reader (Crossroad, 1996)
Girard Reader ch. 6, "Sacrifice as Sacral Violence and Substitution", p. 84

What it does not say
Read in a digital copy of this printed edition; it is not linked because the free copies online are unauthorised uploads of an in-copyright book. The page number is the printed page of the Crossroad edition, read from the page markers of a digital copy and checked against the book's own contents page. A free borrowable scan of the same edition (free account) is at archive.org/details/girardreader0000gira/page/87.

Scholars of ancient religion press a different account, and it has real force. On their account, sacrifice grew from the life of herders and farmers, from killing and sharing domestic animals. Some trace it further back, to the ancient hunt.180 It was about food, about the herd and the meal, about who eats which part. Nothing in the evidence points to a founding murder. And Girard, on this objection, takes a hundred different practices from a hundred different peoples and reads one origin into all of them. Part of that objection should simply be accepted. A single origin for every sacrifice everywhere is more than the evidence can show, and Girard asserted it with more confidence than he could support. Many rites are about the meal, and many offerings were understood as gifts. The herd and the hunt explain what was killed and how. They say less about the word on the Athenian record, purification, and why removing a creature should make a city clean. Other scholars have their own answers to that, from fear of a contagious pollution to magic that moves evil from one body to another. Girard's answer is that the cleanness was the calm of a group that had found its target. So the ruling here is narrow. His theory does not account for every sacrifice. It offers one strong account of the rites of expulsion and purification, beside others that remain live.

180fact
Scholars of ancient religion argue sacrifice grew from the life of herders and farmers, from killing and sharing domestic animals, with some tracing it further to the ancient hunt, against a founding-murder origin.
Jonathan Z. Smith, "The Domestication of Sacrifice", in Robert G. Hamerton-Kelly (ed.), Violent Origins: Walter Burkert, René Girard, and Jonathan Z. Smith on Ritual Killing and Cultural Formation (Stanford UP, 1987)
p. 197

p. 172

How it holds
Smith states the domestication account in his own words, and Burkert names his own hunting derivation as the alternative to Girard's founding murder.
What it does not say
Smith ties sacrifice to herding and farming and finds no clear case among hunters; Burkert traces it back to the hunt. They disagree with each other as well as with Girard. One more objection is older and more common. Religion, on this view, began as early science: thunder needed a thunderer, harvests needed a goddess of grain. That is partly right, and there are gods of weather in every pantheon.181 But it does not explain how much of religion is about violence, purity, pollution and expulsion. Very little of that has anything to do with weather. A theory of religion as the management of a group's own violence expects those things at the centre.

181fact
The older anthropology explained religion as a primitive philosophy of nature, with gods of rain, thunder and grain across cultures.
Edward B. Tylor, Primitive Culture, vol. 2 (London: John Murray, 1871)
p. 169
Read the passage
Edward B. Tylor, Primitive Culture, vol. 2 (London: John Murray, 1871)
p. 249
Read the passage
Edward B. Tylor, Primitive Culture, vol. 2 (London: John Murray, 1871)
p. 278
Read the passage
How it holds
Tylor, the founder of this view, calls animism a philosophy of nature and catalogues rain, thunder and grain deities across cultures.
What it does not say
"Every pantheon" is the script's generalisation; Tylor gives examples across many peoples, not a census.

So here is what stands. The oldest rites look like the scapegoat's peace made regular: a group gathers its trouble onto one creature and removes it. Rules forbid the copying that brought on the crisis. Rites replay the crisis and its ending. Sacrifice puts a substitute where the victim stood, so the peace can be had again without a new lynching. And courts inherit the work. But every story so far has been told by the side that did the expelling. The myths call the victim guilty. The rites never ask. The next thing to find is an old story told from the other side, by the victim or by someone who took his part.

Part 11: Told From the Victim's Side

If myths are the crowd's account of a killing, a story told from the other side would look strange. It would have the same plot as a myth, the same crisis, the same brothers, the same accusation, the same expulsion. And at the decisive moment it would reverse the verdict, and say the victim was innocent and the crowd was guilty. Girard's claim is that such stories exist, and that the Hebrew Bible is full of them. It keeps the plot of the myth and changes the side the story is told from.

There is a common view that this claim runs straight into. Scholars of religion noticed long ago that the Bible shares a great deal with the myths around it.182 A flood, a garden, brothers who quarrel, a hero expelled who returns in glory. The natural conclusion was that the Bible is one more collection of the same old stories, with a different god in charge. The resemblances are real and nobody serious denies them. Girard accepted every resemblance and drew the opposite conclusion. If the plots match, then the place to look is where they part. And where they part, in the cases Girard chose, is in the same place: the verdict on the victim.

182fact
Comparative study of myth found many myths resembling the Gospel story, and many concluded Christianity was one more myth.
René Girard, "Are the Gospels Mythical?", First Things 62 (April 1996)
opening paragraphs
Read the passage
How it holds
Girard opens the essay by stating the comparativists' case at its strongest before replying to it.
What it does not say
Girard is describing the view he answers, and he speaks of the Gospels and the Passion; the script's list (flood, garden, brothers) is its own.

Start with two founding stories that differ on one thing only. Rome begins with twin brothers, Romulus and Remus, quarrelling over where to build a city. The historian who tells the story gives two versions. In the first, Remus is struck down in a brawl among their followers, and nobody is named as the one who struck him. In the second, Remus mocks his brother by leaping over the half-built wall, and Romulus kills him. So it will go, Romulus says, for anyone else who leaps over his walls.183 Either way, Rome is founded, and the killing is part of the founding. Girard's summary of the Roman verdict is short: "So Romulus is justified".184 The murder of Remus, he writes, is told as an act perhaps to be regretted, but justified by the victim's transgression. Remus crossed a line. He had it coming. That is the voice of the side that won, though later Roman poets would call the killing their city's founding crime185.

183fact
Livy gives two versions of Remus's death: killed in a brawl among followers with no one named as killer, or killed by Romulus for mocking him by leaping the city wall.
Livy, Ab Urbe Condita 1.7.2-3 (Latin text, The Latin Library)
1.7.2-3
Read the passage
How it holds
Read in Latin from the Latin Library; both versions (brawl, and the wall-leaping mockery) are in 1.7.2-3.
What it does not say
Livy's Latin for Romulus's threat has no verb; the familiar English 'So perish whoever else...' is a translator's wording (Roberts, not read in this research). The 'nobody is named as the killer' detail belongs to Livy's first version; Girard is not confirmed to comment on this specific detail.

Now the second founding story. Two brothers again, Cain and Abel. A rivalry again, this time over whose offering is accepted. "Cain rose up against Abel his brother, and slew him".186 And then Cain goes out and builds the first city in the Bible.187 The plot is the Roman plot: brothers, rivalry, a killing, a city. Even the doubles are there, as the earlier steps would predict. What differs is a single sentence. God says to Cain, "the voice of thy brother's blood crieth unto me from the ground".188 The dead brother has a voice, and the voice accuses. The story gives Abel no transgression at all. Cain, in Girard's words, "is nonetheless presented as a vulgar murderer", and the city he founds is founded on a crime.189

Set them side by side and the difference is the whole point. Same structure, opposite verdict. The Roman story Girard used says the brother deserved it. Genesis says the blood cries out. Girard called Abel the "first in a long line of victims whom the Bible exhumes and exonerates".190 To exhume is to dig up a body that was buried. That is what the story does to the founding murder that the Roman version leaves underground.

190quotation

Girard Reader ch. 10, p. 151 (Things Hidden, Book II ch. 1, p. 149)

The second pair is longer and sharper, because it runs along the plot of Oedipus almost step by step. Joseph is the favourite son of his father, and his brothers hate him for it. The text says plainly that "they hated him, and could not speak peaceably unto him". They plot to kill him, then sell him instead to traders bound for Egypt.191 That is the expulsion: one young man driven out by his brothers, with only the eldest trying to save him.192 In Egypt he rises in a great man's household, and the great man's wife tries to seduce him. He refuses and runs, and she keeps the cloak he left behind. Then she accuses him of attacking her.193 It is the kind of accusation the plague poem and the Oedipus story both carry. It is a crime against the order of a household, the sort of crime that makes the accused a monster. Joseph goes to prison.

How it holds
39:9 shows his refusal, 39:17 shows the false charge ('came in unto me to mock me'), 39:20 shows the imprisonment.

Here is where the two stories part. In the Oedipus story the accusation is treated as true; the myth confirms the charge and the hero accepts it. In the Joseph story the reader has watched the whole scene. The text shows him refusing and shows him fleeing. The charge is false, and the story makes certain everyone knows it. Girard's comment: "The biblical text, unlike the Oedipus myth, disbelieves this accusation".194 Then Joseph rises again, becomes the second man in Egypt, and in a famine his brothers come to him for grain without recognising him. He tests them. He threatens to keep the youngest brother as a prisoner, and one of the brothers who sold Joseph offers to stay in the youngest one's place.195 The brother who once helped expel a victim now offers himself instead of a victim. Then Joseph reveals himself. "I am Joseph your brother, whom ye sold into Egypt".196 He forgives them.
