One line at the end of the story looks at first like the myth's own move. Joseph tells his brothers, "ye thought evil against me; but God meant it unto good".197 That can sound like the old logic: the expulsion worked out, so it was meant. But read the first half of the sentence again. The brothers' act is called evil, without qualification. The myth's move is to find the victim guilty and the expulsion right. Here the victim is innocent, the act is evil, and good comes out of it anyway. That is a different claim, and it leaves the brothers no cover.

197quotation
Joseph tells his brothers: ye thought evil against me, but God meant it unto good.
Genesis 50, King James Version
Gen 50:20
Read the passage
What it does not say
Gen 50:20 can sound like the myth's own justifying move (the expulsion 'meant for good'). The honest difference, which the script itself names, is that the text never calls Joseph guilty and calls the brothers' act 'evil' in the same verse.

The third case is the starkest, because it is almost nothing but the accusation. Job loses his children, his wealth and his health. His friends come to sit with him, and then, one after another, they explain why it happened. He must have done something. God does not punish the innocent. The book frames their speeches as a debate about why the innocent suffer.198 Girard read them as a crowd's verdict in small, all pointing one way. Job describes what it is like to be on the other side of it. "All my inward friends abhorred me: and they whom I loved are turned against me".199 He says he has become a song and a byword.200 And Job refuses to agree. That refusal is what sets him apart. The victims of the old mechanism agreed with their persecutors; in the first play, Oedipus confesses and asks to be cast out.201 In a later play he protests that he acted in ignorance, but the first play's verdict is the myth's.202 Job does the opposite. "Till I die I will not remove mine integrity from me".203 At the end of the book, God turns to the friends. He tells them, "ye have not spoken of me the thing that is right, as my servant Job hath".204 The unanimous accusers are wrong, and the lone accused is right.

One honest complication belongs here. Job does not only blame his friends. He also blames God, saying that God has put his brothers far from him.205 The book does not resolve that cleanly, and it would be a cheat to pretend it does. What it does settle is the friends' verdict. Whatever God's part, the crowd's account of Job's guilt is declared false by the voice the book trusts most.

205fact
Job also blames God, saying God has put his brothers far from him.
Job 19, King James Version
Job 19:13
Read the passage
What it does not say
The book does not resolve this cleanly; Girard's reading selects the friends' verdict and leans on other chapters (16, 19, 29-30), which is itself a fair complication the script names.

Now the objection, and it is serious. The same book that exonerates Abel and Joseph and Job also commands the opposite. In a war of conquest it orders that the people save alive "nothing that breatheth".206 Its law prescribes death by stoning. In one passage the instruction is to "let all the congregation stone him", the unanimous killing written into law.207 The goat loaded with the people's sins and driven into the wilderness is this book's law too.208 So a story told from the victim's side also carries the crowd's side, in its own commandments. Girard did not dodge this. He conceded that in these texts "Yahweh is still the god to whom vengeance belongs".209 A fair verdict has to hold both facts. The Hebrew Bible contains the mechanism and its exposure side by side, sometimes in the same chapter. What is new is that the exposures are there at all, and that they keep returning, from Abel to Joseph to Job. Girard held that no myth contains one. Critics answer with the trial of Socrates, told by his friends as an innocent man condemned by his city.210 That is a fair point against him. The exposures in these books are many and deliberate, and that is the claim that survives.

Read the passage

So the common view is half kept and half discarded. Kept: the Bible shares its plots with the myths of its neighbours. Discarded: the idea that sharing plots makes it the same kind of story. A myth tells the founding murder as the victim's fault. These stories tell it as the crowd's crime. Abel's blood speaks, Joseph's accuser is shown lying, and Job's friends are told they were wrong. On Girard's reading, the exposure in each case is partial. Even Cain, after the murder, is protected by a threat of sevenfold vengeance.211 Joseph's story is a family's, not a whole people's. Job's friends are rebuked, but they are three men, not a city.

211fact
Even Cain, after the murder, is protected by a threat of sevenfold vengeance.
Genesis 4, King James Version
Gen 4:15
Read the passage

One prophet comes closer, with a servant "despised and rejected of men".212 What sets the poem apart is who is speaking. The speakers are a "we", the people who watched him suffer and turned their faces away.213 First they confess what they used to think: "we did esteem him stricken, smitten of God, and afflicted".214 That is the myth's verdict in the crowd's own mouth: he suffers, so heaven must be against him. Then they take it back. "But he was wounded for our transgressions, he was bruised for our iniquities".215 And the poem says outright that "he had done no violence, neither was any deceit in his mouth".216 A crowd that despised a man, and believed God had struck him, tells the story of its own change of mind. The complication runs through the same poem. A few verses on, "it pleased the Lord to bruise him". God still stands behind the violence, and Girard granted it: here "God himself is presented as the principal instigator of the persecution".217 The poem is halfway out of the mechanism, and only halfway. And even there the crowd's turning is remembered in a poem, not shown as it happens. One Greek play does show the whole mechanism on stage, from the crisis to the killing. The question is what it does with the crowd's verdict.

Part 12: The God of the Crowd

Socrates was the easy counter-case, an innocent man condemned by his city and mourned by his friends. There is a far harder one, and a sceptic would put it like this. Centuries before the Gospels were written, a Greek play showed a whole crowd tearing one man apart. It showed the killing as horror, and it brought the killer, step by step, to see what she had done. If a pagan stage could do that, the claim made for the Gospels looks like special pleading. The play is Euripides' Bacchae, and the sceptic is right about almost everything in it. The play sees the whole mechanism. It then lays the killers' deed on a god and leaves the victim guilty, and the second of those moves is the one the Gospels refuse.

The play opens on a crisis of differences, staged as clearly as anything before it. The god Dionysus has come to Thebes, and the city's women have gone up the mountain as his worshippers. Then two old men get ready to join them. One is the city's founder. The other is its blind seer. The founder asks whether they should ride up to the mountain in a chariot. The seer says no, because then "the god would not have equal honor".218 As for their age, he says, "the god has made no distinction as to whether it is right for men young or old to dance".219 That is the old speech about an army without ranks, played on a stage. Take but degree away, and the founder and the seer go up the mountain like everyone else. Rank and age were the distinctions keeping people out of each other's way, and the god has dissolved both of them.

218quotation
Cadmus asks whether they should go up to the mountain by chariot; Teiresias says no, because then the god would not have equal honor.
Euripides, Bacchae, trans. T. A. Buckley (1850), Perseus Digital Library
ll. 191-192 (Kadmos asks, Teiresias answers)
Read the passage
219quotation
Teiresias says the god has made no distinction of age in who should dance.
Euripides, Bacchae, trans. T. A. Buckley (1850), Perseus Digital Library
ll. 206-207 (Teiresias)
Read the passage
What it does not say
extend-bacchae.md first placed this at ll. 204-205; extend-curl.md's character-by-character re-check corrects it to ll. 206-207 (speaker Teiresias confirmed in both passes).

Next come the doubles, and here the god is one of them. Dionysus says in his first lines that his mother was the founder's daughter.220 The young king of Thebes, Pentheus, is the son of another of the founder's daughters. So by the family lines, the god and the king who refuses him are cousins.221 The quarrel is a family quarrel from the start. The god complains that "my mother's sisters, the ones who least should", denied that he was the son of Zeus.222 The king fights the god, and the fight ends with the king dressed as one of the god's own worshippers. In that costume he looks out and says, "I think I see two suns, and twin Thebes, the seven-gated city". The god answers, "Now you see what you should see".223 Recall the doubles, two rivals nobody can tell apart in the fight. The king has become the follower he hated, and the world in front of him has split in two.

Then the killing, told by a messenger, and nothing in it is hidden. The women find the king up in a pine tree. "They applied countless hands to the pine and dragged it up from the earth".224 He falls among them. "His mother, as priestess, began the slaughter".225 He knows her, and he begs. "It is I, mother, your son, Pentheus". "Pity me, mother, and do not kill me, your child, for my sins". Even in his plea he grants that he has sinned, which is how the victims of the old mechanism talk.226 She does not hear him. She "was possessed by Bacchus, and he did not persuade her". Bacchus is another name for the same god.227 Then comes the sentence that matters most. "She tore out his shoulder, not by her own strength, but the god gave facility to her hands".228 Her sisters join in, and "the whole crowd of the Bacchae pressed on". Every hand, one body.229 The war of all against one is on the record of the text, from the first hand on the tree to the last.

Now ask what a killer in a crowd sees. The obvious guess is that she sees a man, and hates him. The play gives a stranger answer. The mother fixes her son's head on the end of her staff, and the messenger says she carries it like the head of a savage lion. She comes back to the city in triumph. "I caught this young wild lion cub without snares", she says.230 Then she turns to the women of the chorus and says, "Share in the feast then". They recoil: "What? I share in the feast, wretched woman?"231 The guess was hatred, and the answer is blindness. The crowd never sees its own hand. Here a mother holds her own son's head and sees a hunting trophy. The crowd does not know whom it has killed, and the play shows it not knowing, in the person who should know best.

230quotation

Then comes the scene that makes this the strongest case against Girard's claim. Her father, the old founder, does not argue with her. He asks plain questions. "First cast your eye up to this sky". She says it is "brighter than before and more translucent". Something in her is already clearing. "To whose house did you come in marriage?" She names her husband. "What son did you bear to your husband in the house?" Pentheus, she says. "Whose head do you hold in your hands?" And she answers, "A lion's, as they who hunted him down said".232 Her last defence is the crowd's word: the lion is a lion because the others said so. He tells her to examine it correctly, because "it takes but little effort to see".233 She looks. "I see the greatest grief, wretched that I am". And then, "I, wretched, hold the head of Pentheus".234

That has to be granted in full. A persecutor, carried along by a crowd and blind to her victim, is brought to see him. It happens on a Greek stage, long before the Gospels. This counter-case is far stronger than Socrates, because here the crowd itself is shown, and the one who sees is one of the killers. The scapegoat mechanism was not invisible to the Greeks.

The question is what the play does once the killer can see. And here a reading has to be declared, once. The play has been read both as piety toward the god and as an attack on him. The text itself holds both, the god's verdict and the protest against it, side by side, and both are taken here as written.

The humans come first, because they are clear-sighted and still keep the verdict. The founder tells his daughter, "You and your sisters killed him". That is the killers named, by their own father, with no god in the sentence. But in the same breath he gives the reason. "He went to mock the god and your revelry". The victim provoked it.235 Then the god himself appears and justifies everything. Had they known how to be wise, he tells them, "you would have acquired Zeus' son as an ally, and would now be happy".236 The founder begs him and confesses that the family has acted unjustly. So the survivors accept their guilt toward the god, and the king's guilt along with it.237 The god's own reason is one line: "I, a god by birth, was insulted by you".238

The protest is real, and all of it comes from the old founder. Even before the god appears, he says the god has destroyed them "justly, yet too severely".239 Facing the god, he says, "Now we know, but you go too far against us".240 And then the play's sharpest line: "Gods should not resemble mortals in their anger". The god does not argue. "My father Zeus approved this long ago".241 The protest is heard, and the verdict stands.

Euripides, Bacchae, trans. T. A. Buckley (1850), Perseus Digital Library
l. 1349 (Dionysus)
Read the passage
What it does not say
Speaker of the 'gods should not resemble mortals' line is Kadmos, not Agave (extend-bacchae.md caution).

So the play does three things at once, and each one can be pointed to. First, it shows the horror: a mother tearing at her son while he begs, and a crowd pressing on. Second, it lays the crowd's deed on a god, though the killers still carry the guilt. Her hands had his strength, not her own; she was possessed, and he did not persuade her. Third, it keeps the victim partly guilty, along with his whole family. He mocked the god, the family concedes it acted unjustly, and the only complaint left is that the punishment went too far. Justly, yet too severely. That is the founder's ruling on his whole house, and the god lets it stand. The crowd is shown, the killer sees, and the verdict on the victim survives the seeing.

The Gospels also say the crowd did not know what it was doing. On that point the play and the Gospels agree, and the play said it first. A sceptic can push further, and should. The Gospels also put a power behind the killing. The Gospels say Satan entered the disciple who betrayed him. The book of Acts says the death came by "the determinate counsel and foreknowledge of God".242 Girard reads that Satan as the crowd's own accusing, not a person. But the same reading is open for the god of the Bacchae, whom Girard himself calls "the god of decisive mob action".243 So putting a power behind the killing does not separate the two texts cleanly. The verdict on the victim does. The play lets the victim deserve some of what he got. The Gospels never say he had it coming, even a little. So the claim for the Gospels gets smaller, and it gets sharper. The Greeks did not lack the sight. What the Gospels add is the verdict on the victim, kept clean through the whole story.

242quotation
The Gospels say Satan entered the disciple who betrayed Jesus; Acts says the death came by the determinate counsel and foreknowledge of God.
The Holy Bible, King James Version, Gospel of Luke
Luke 22:3
Read the passage
The Holy Bible, King James Version, Acts
Acts 2:23
Read the passage
243quotation
Girard himself calls Dionysus the god of decisive mob action.
René Girard, Violence and the Sacred, trans. Patrick Gregory (Continuum, 2005 reprint), ch. 5 'Dionysus'
ch. 5, p. 142
print copy
What it does not say
Pages are the Continuum 2005 reprint's, not the 1977 Johns Hopkins edition's. Read in the publisher's own preview of this printed edition (Google Books in-volume search, which shows the sentence with its printed page); not linked, because that search page does not reliably show the words to every visitor.

One reader saw this pairing more clearly than anyone, and took the other side on purpose. The philosopher Nietzsche set the torn god against the crucified one in his notes. "Dionysus versus 'Christ'; here you have the contrast". "It is not a difference in regard to the martyrdom, but the latter has a different meaning". Same suffering, different meaning.244 That is the Cain and Abel formula, same plot and opposite verdict, said by the theory's strongest opponent. And his sympathy shows in the words he chose. "God on the Cross is a curse upon Life", he wrote. "Dionysus cut into pieces is a promise of Life". On his view, a story that sides with the innocent victim turns suffering into an accusation against life itself. The torn god says the opposite, that life "will be for ever born anew, and rise afresh from destruction".245 That is the objection at full strength. Part of it will come back in the modern world, where siding with victims turns into a new kind of crowd.

244quotation
Nietzsche set Dionysus against Christ in his notes: same suffering, a different meaning.
Friedrich Nietzsche, The Will to Power, vol. II, trans. A. M. Ludovici, Project Gutenberg 52915
§1052 ('The two types; Dionysus and Christ on the Cross.')
Read the passage
Friedrich Nietzsche, The Will to Power, vol. II, trans. A. M. Ludovici, Project Gutenberg 52915

Nietzsche: God on the Cross is a curse on life; Dionysus cut to pieces is a promise that life is reborn.
Friedrich Nietzsche, The Will to Power, vol. II, trans. A. M. Ludovici, Project Gutenberg 52915

One sentence joined by a dash in the original (extend-bacchae.md); quoted here as the script splits it across clauses.

A Greek play, then, shows a crisis of differences, two doubles, a crowd that kills one man, and a killer brought to see. The Greeks saw the mechanism, and nothing in the Gospels' favour should deny it. But the play leaves the victim guilty, only less guilty than his punishment. It passes the first half of the test and fails the second. The next test is whether any text shows the whole mechanism, from crisis to crowd to killing, and refuses the crowd's verdict on the victim throughout.

Part 13: The Crowd at the Cross

The story of the crucifixion has the structure of every founding murder. There is a crisis and a crowd that becomes unanimous. There are authorities who make peace with each other over the victim, and a death that is supposed to save the nation. But it is told by his followers, decades later. It calls the victim innocent and says the crowd does not know what it is doing. Girard's claim is that a mechanism which works only while nobody sees it loses its innocence once it is told this way. It can still run, but never again without someone able to name it. That claim is the hinge of his whole theory, and it is also where he has been most fiercely accused of special pleading.

The crisis comes first, and the story states it in so many words. Jerusalem is under foreign occupation, crowded for a festival, and the authorities fear an uprising that would bring the occupying army down on everyone. In John's telling, the high priest tells the council how to think about the problem. "It is expedient for us, that one man should die for the people, and that the whole nation perish not".246 That sentence is the scapegoat mechanism spoken aloud. It contains no hatred. It is calculation: one death against many. On Girard's reading, every such crowd acts on it without knowing it. Here it is said aloud. Nobody in the story is running the mechanism like a machine operator; a frightened council reasons about a crisis, and the crowd does the rest. The council sees the convenience of one death; it does not see that the guilt will be supplied by the crowd.

246quotation
In John's telling, Caiaphas tells the council it is better for one man to die for the people than for the whole nation to perish.
The Holy Bible, King James Version, Gospel of John
John 11:49-50, 53
Read the passage
How it holds
v.48 (not separately quoted) gives the motive: fear that Rome will destroy the nation.
What it does not say
Girard's own reading of Caiaphas as reasonable politics, not malice (The Scapegoat ch. 10), was found only as a catalogue record, not read, and is not voiced here; the script's framing that the council 'does not see' what the crowd will do is the script's own reading.

Then the crowd. A few days earlier, crowds in the same city had welcomed this man in. Now a crowd before the governor cries, "Crucify him". The governor, Pilate, sees no crime and says so, and then gives way anyway, "willing to content the people".247 Girard pointed out what the speed of the change implies. A crowd that cheers a man on one day and demands his death a few days later is not expressing a settled judgement. In his words, "rather than a deep-seated hatred for the victim, it suggests a wave of contagious violence".248 It is the snowball of accusation, the war of all against one, set down by the victim's followers.

247quotation
A crowd before Pilate cries Crucify him, and Pilate, willing to content the people, hands Jesus over.
The Holy Bible, King James Version, Gospel of Mark
Mark 15:11-15
Read the passage

Mark 15:15
Read the passage

Girard reads the same crowd's cheering then crying for death as a wave of contagious violence, not a settled hatred.

online text, paragraph on the crowd before Pilate

What it does not say
The link is the essay as First Things published it; that is its first publication, so the page is Girard's own text, not a summary of it.

Two details show how complete the unanimity becomes. The first concerns the rulers. Pilate sends the prisoner to Herod, the local king, and Herod sends him back. And Luke adds a sentence in which Girard saw the whole mechanism. "The same day Pilate and Herod were made friends together: for before they were at enmity between themselves".249 Two rivals are reconciled by passing a victim between them. Girard's comment is short: "It is a cathartic effect rooted in the mimetic contagion".250 The peace the victim brings is already at work before he dies. The second detail is the closest friend. Peter, who swore he would die with his master, stands by a fire in the high priest's courtyard among the servants and guards. A servant girl says he was with the prisoner. "Woman, I know him not", he says, three times.251 He has not stopped loving his master. But in Girard's reading, "When surrounded by people hostile to Jesus, he imitates their hostility".252 Even the disciple is drawn into the crowd. Nobody, in the end, stands with the victim.

online text, paragraph on the disciples' failure
Read the passage
What it does not say
The link is the essay as First Things published it; that is its first publication, so the page is Girard's own text, not a summary of it.

So far this is a founding murder, complete in every part. A crisis threatens the nation. A leader states the logic of one death for many. A crowd becomes unanimous by contagion. Rivals reconcile over the victim. Even the victim's friends abandon him. If the story stopped here and were told by the crowd, it would be a myth. Its hero might in time have become a god of the city.

Here is where it parts from every myth, and from the best of the tragedies. The story is told by the side that lost. It says plainly that the accusations were false and that the witnesses did not agree. And it gives the victim a sentence about his killers that is the exact opposite of the crowd's verdict. "Father, forgive them; for they know not what they do".253 In the Greek play, the crowd's blindness came with a guilty victim. Here it comes with an innocent one. Girard insisted that this be read literally, as a description. He pointed to a parallel line later in the same writings, where the crowd is told it acted in ignorance.254 The very next verse adds that God so fulfilled what the prophets foretold.255 So here too a god stands behind the death, and what stays different is the verdict on the victim. The persecutors are not wicked masterminds. They do not know what they are doing, because the mechanism works only when nobody knows. Girard summed up the reversal this way: "Instead of blaming victimization on the victims, the Gospels blame it on the victimizers".256 Same plot. Opposite verdict. And this time, the verdict is passed on the whole crowd, including the reader's own representative, the disciple who denied.

253quotation
From the cross Jesus says: Father, forgive them, for they know not what they do.
The Holy Bible, King James Version, Gospel of Luke
Luke 23:34

Now the strangest move in the Gospels, and Girard built a great deal on it. In these scriptures Satan is named "the accuser of our brethren", the one who turns people against someone.257 Jesus is accused of driving out demons by the power of Satan. He answers with a question: "How can Satan cast out Satan?"258 The question sounds like a riddle. Girard read it as a description of the mechanism. What kind of disorder can expel disorder? His answer: "the answer is unanimous victimization", everyone turning on one.259 The violence of a crowd ends the violence of the crowd. Disorder, turned against one person, produces order. On this reading, Satan is not a horned figure but the process itself, the accuser who gathers everyone against one. And the kingdom of Satan stands, Jesus says, only so long as it is not divided against itself.260 That is to say, only so long as the crowd is unanimous. A story that breaks the unanimity, by taking the victim's side, breaks the kingdom.

The Gospels also call this death a sacrifice, and that word can mean two opposite things. An old court case shows both. Two women come before King Solomon. They live in one house, and there was "no stranger with us in the house, save we two in the house".261 Each had a baby, one baby died in the night, and each claims the living child. Their claims are mirror images: "the living is my son, and the dead is thy son", says one, and the other says the same words back. They are doubles in the strict sense, with nothing to tell them apart.262 The king calls for a sword and orders the living child cut in two, half to each.263 The two women answer in opposite ways. One cries out, "O my lord, give her the living child, and in no wise slay it". The text gives her reason: "her bowels yearned upon her son".264 The other says, "Let it be neither mine nor thine, but divide it".265 The second answer is the old solution, a victim cut in two so that the rivals come out equal and the quarrel ends. The first is a claim given up so that a life can go on. The king gives the child to the one who was willing to lose it.266 Both answers could be called a sacrifice, and they are opposites. For many Christians, the cross is a sacrifice in the old sense: a death God required as payment. Girard first rejected the word sacrifice for it, then took the word back in another sense: a life given up rather than a victim taken.267 That is the first mother's sense, not the second's. Girard held that this sense fits the story's own verdict, which is that the killing was a crime.

Now the objection a sceptic presses hardest. This looks like Christianity congratulating itself. A Christian scholar finds that every religion but his own is founded on a lie, and his own on the truth. Worse, the history is against him. Recall the plague poem. Machaut's world was Christian. The crowds that burned the Jews of Europe were Christian crowds. In Strasbourg, the Jews who would not be baptised were burned in the plague year.268 Christians, with the story of the innocent victim in their hands, ran the old mechanism at full strength. And the charge they used came partly from that story. It lays the guilt on the Jewish crowd and its leaders, and lets the Roman governor off.269

How it holds

What it does not say
The Council's text shows the charge was drawn from the Passion story ("as if this followed from the Holy Scriptures"); how much of medieval hostility came from it rather than other sources is not measured here. Other Gospels place the guilt differently.

That objection deserves a two-part answer, and the first part is a concession. The church as an institution has very often been the crowd. Nothing in Girard's theory denies that, and any version of it that does is false. The fault is in the text too. It lays the crowd's guilt on one people and spares the governor, the same kind of shift held against the Greek play. What survives is smaller. The story's structure condemns the crowd, any crowd, and Girard's claim is that later readers turned that structure against the burners. The second part is the reason the text still matters. Think what the modern reader does with the Strasbourg chronicle. The reader condemns the burning, at once, without needing to be told. On his reading, the standard comes from the Passion story: the crowd was wrong, the victims innocent, the accusation false. The persecutors, as he saw it, carried a story that condemned them, and in the end it did. A revelation can be owned and betrayed by the same people. What it cannot do, once it is told, is be untold.

The second objection is one Girard largely granted. The Hebrew Bible did this first. Abel's blood, Joseph's false accuser and Job's wrong friends all came before. Girard said so himself: "Beginning with the story of Cain and Abel, the Bible proclaims the innocence of mythical victims".270 His claim for the Gospels is narrower than it sounds. They show the whole mechanism, from crisis to unanimous crowd to killing, and never let the crowd's verdict on the victim stand. In his words, "The Gospels make the revelation complete". Completion, not invention.271 Calling it completion is Girard's Christian ranking, and a Jewish reader can answer that Abel, Joseph and Job need nothing completed.

The Passion story, then, has the full structure of a founding murder. It differs from a myth in one decisive way: it is told from the victim's side, and it calls the crowd ignorant and the victim innocent. A mechanism that depends on nobody seeing it loses its innocence once shown, though not its power. Christians often betrayed the story, yet on Girard's reading the standard by which we condemn them runs through it. In the end, on his reading, the story condemned them. That claim about the long run can sound like faith. The fair question is whether it has ever been seen at the scale of one town, with names and dates.

Part 14: The Town That Took It Back

The claim that the persecutors' own story would condemn them has so far rested on a reading of history. There is one small place where it can be watched, in the persecutors' own handwriting. In 1692 a Christian town in Massachusetts ran the old mechanism, and its court became part of the crowd. Then, within one generation, its jurors, a judge and a chief accuser said in public and in writing that they had been deluded, and asked pardon. That is the turn the theory predicts. It came late, and twenty people were dead before it took hold.

The four marks can be read straight off the record of how it began. In Salem Village, people who said they were afflicted began crying out that neighbours were tormenting them by witchcraft. Why the first accusers accused is still disputed, and nothing in the argument needs it settled. What matters is who they named first. A critic who wrote the affair's history soon afterwards listed them. One was a servant of the minister's household, an Indian woman, who lay in prison afterwards until she was sold to pay her jail fees.272 One had "long been counted a Melancholy or Distracted Woman". The third was "an Old Bed-rid Woman". The second and third, he added, were "Persons so ill thought of" that the charge was all the more readily believed.273 The rest can be counted without help. A village frightened by suffering it could not explain. A crime that attacks the order of everything: witchcraft. Victims chosen by marks, the ones least able to answer. And then the rope. The signs fit those three women. Within months the accusations reached Rebecca Nurse, a respected member of the church, and no sign explains her.

Now the court, because this is where the case tests something said much earlier. A court ends a feud only because it stands above both sides. The Salem court admitted a kind of testimony called spectral evidence. An accuser swore that the accused person's spectre, a ghostly double nobody else could see, had come and attacked her. No one could test that claim, and no one could answer it.274 It was the crowd's own conviction, entered into the record as proof. So what happens when a jury says not guilty anyway? Most people would assume the verdict stands, since that is what a verdict is. Here is what happened to Rebecca Nurse, a respected old woman.

274fact
The Salem court admitted spectral evidence: an accuser's sworn claim that the accused's invisible 'spectre' had attacked her, a claim no one else could see or test.
Sir William Phips, letter to the home government, February 21, 1693, in Robert Calef, More Wonders of the Invisible World (London, 1700), Part V, in George Lincoln Burr (ed.), Narratives of the Witchcraft Cases, 1648-1706 (Scribner, 1914)

Her jury brought her in not guilty. At once, the critic wrote, "all the accusers in the Court, and suddenly after all the afflicted out of Court, made an hideous out-cry".275 One of the judges said he was not satisfied. The chief judge said "he would not Impose upon the Jury", and then pointed them to something she had said in court.276 That hint, "together with the Clamours of the Accusers", sent the jury out again. They came back and brought her in guilty.277 Even that was not the end. The governor granted her a reprieve. The accusers "renewed their dismal out-cries against her", the reprieve was recalled, and she was hanged with the others.278 Twice the institution meant to stand above the crowd gave its judgment. Twice the crowd's noise, with men of standing behind it, overturned it. So the court's power has a condition the earlier argument did not state. A court stands above the antagonists only while its evidence comes from outside them. Once it takes the accusers' certainty as proof, its verdict is the crowd's verdict with a seal on it. None of the judges, on the record, planned this.

The strangest feature of the record comes next. The people who confessed to witchcraft had admitted the crime, and they were the ones who lived. Many confessed, the critic wrote, "yet not one of them that confessed their own guilt, and abode by their Confession were put to Death".279 Those last words carry the case. One man confessed, then took the confession back. He was tried on "his former Confession and Spectre Testimony", and he was hanged.280 Other confessors in prison were told they would follow him.281 So confession kept a prisoner alive only for as long as the prisoner kept agreeing. Recanting the lie could kill you. Another man pleaded not guilty but refused to be tried by the jury, because, as the critic put it, "they having cleared none upon Tryal". He was pressed to death under weights, the first in New England.282 By the autumn of 1692 nineteen people had been hanged and one pressed to death.283

Oedipus confessed and the myth stayed safe; Job refused and the friends' verdict cracked. Salem sorted its prisoners by that line. Everyone who confessed and held to it escaped the rope, and every one of the twenty who died had denied, or taken a confession back. The mechanism rewards agreeing with it, because a victim who agrees confirms the crowd, and a victim who refuses is a witness against it. The people Salem killed were its Jobs.

Then how it stopped. The pope's argument from evidence had changed nothing in the plague year. In Massachusetts an argument was made in 1693, and this time the hangings stopped. One of the colony's ministers published a book against spectral evidence. Its premise was that the devil may, by divine permission, "appear in the shape of Innocent and Pious Persons".284 So a spectre proves nothing about the person it resembles. And it stated a rule: "It were better that ten suspected Witches should escape, than that one innocent Person should be Condemned".285 That rule does not argue about the evidence alone. It argues from the side of the person who might be innocent, and it accepts guilty people going free as the price. The governor stopped the court, he wrote, "because I saw many innocent persons might otherwise perish". His letter credits the ministers' judgment, and says spectral evidence "had not the same stress layd upon them as before". It was set aside, not abolished.286 At the new court, about fifty people were tried, three were convicted, and none of the three was hanged.287

The other causes are real, and they belong here too. The governor's own letter says the affair had touched "the reputacion of some of the principall persons here".288 There was even talk, which the critic himself marks as hearsay, that the governor's own wife had been named.289 Once the accusations reached the leading families, the town stopped agreeing. The church stood on both sides. The first accused came out of a minister's household, and that servant later said her master beat her to make her confess and accuse. Rebecca Nurse was put out of her church after her conviction.290 Yet the argument that stopped the hangings also came from a minister. Salem's ministers were the crowd and the brake at once.

What the town did next is the part that matters most, and it was written down. A few years later, twelve of the trial jurors signed a declaration. It says, "we justly fear that we were sadly deluded and mistaken".291 The evidence they had acted on, they wrote, "we justly fear was insufficient for the touching the Lives of any". They feared they had brought on themselves and the people "the Guilt of Innocent Blood".292 They asked forgiveness first of God, and then "of you all, whom we have justly offended", meaning above all the surviving sufferers.293 One of the judges went further. On a fast day he handed a written bill to the minister in church and stood while it was read aloud. In it he desired "to take the Blame and shame of it, Asking pardon of men".294

Then in August 1706 one of the chief accusers, by then a grown woman, was received into the village church. Her written confession was read by the pastor before the seated congregation, while she stood in her place.295 She said she had been made "an instrument for the accusing of several persons of a grievous crime, whereby their lives were taken away from them".296 She said she was "a chief instrument of accusing of Goodwife Nurse and her two sisters", the woman the jury had once acquitted.297 And she named what had moved her. She also says, "I did it not out of any anger, malice, or ill-will".299 She was not a schemer confessing a scheme. She describes being carried along by something she did not see while she was inside it, which is what the theory says a crowd is.

The limit comes first. Salem does not prove that the Passion story caused the retraction. The legal change, the fear among the leading families and the private consciences of jurors are all real causes, and the record cannot pull them apart. What it does show is smaller, and it is enough. A persecuting Christian town came to judge its own persecution by the standard the theory traces to the cross. The crowd was wrong, the victims were innocent, and the accusers did not know what they were doing. It reached that judgment within one generation, in the words of the people who did the killing. The jurors' own phrase, the guilt of innocent blood, is the Hebrew law's, which on Girard's reading the cross completes rather than replaces.300 That is what the claim that the persecutors' story would condemn them looks like, at the size of one town. Salem also shows the old cure failing. Twenty killings brought no calm, and the accusing spread. By one letter of the time, it went on until members of the governor's council and justices were accused.301 Twenty people died before the refusal took hold. It took hold when an argument from the side of the possibly innocent met accusations reaching the governor's own circle. And Salem is chosen here because it took it back, which is exactly why one town cannot stand for all the others.

Three things are now on record. The mechanism ran in a Christian town, with every mark in place at the start. A court that admitted the crowd's certainty as evidence became the crowd, and overturned its own acquittal. And the persecutors themselves came to see what they had done, in the victim's language, and signed it. One town's retraction is a single case. And that raises the question of what happens to a world that has seen the machine. If the old cure for violence no longer works, something has to happen to the violence.

Part 15: The Concern for Victims

The modern world, Girard argued, cares about victims in a way no society before it ever did. His claim is that this is the revelation of the Passion story, at work in history. It has robbed the old scapegoat of its innocence. But it has not ended rivalry, so the rivalry has found a new language: persecution now speaks in the victim's name. And with the old brake gone, nothing automatic stops rivalry from escalating. The choice the mechanism used to make for us has become ours.

His observation is stranger than it looks. Girard put it without hedging. "No historical period, no society we know, has ever spoken of victims as we do".302 Ancient cities pitied particular sufferers, and every religion has its charities. What is new is that being a victim has become a source of moral authority. Courts, newspapers, governments and movements all compete to be seen on the victim's side. A modern politician accused of creating victims is in trouble. An ancient king accused of it would not have understood the charge. Whether the Passion story is the cause is another matter. Other sources have a claim: the philosophers' idea of equal human dignity, traditions of compassion far from Christianity, the rights of man. The answer open to Girard is that the modern versions of those ideals grew inside a culture already soaked in the story. That is a claim about historical causes, and nobody can run the experiment. It should be held as his reading of history, not as a result.

302quotation
Girard: no historical period, no society we know, has ever spoken of victims as we do.
René Girard, I See Satan Fall Like Lightning, trans. James G. Williams (Orbis, 2001), ch. 13 'The Modern Concern for Victims'
ch. 13, "The Modern Concern for Victims", p. 161
print copy
How it holds

Here is one small sign of how deep this goes, and it is in the very word scapegoat. To say that someone is being scapegoated is, in modern speech, an accusation. It means: the crowd is wrong, the person is being blamed for what is not their fault. The Athenian rite had no such sentence in it. For them the man led out of the city was a purification.303 For us, the phrase that names him names an injustice. The word itself now carries the victim's side of the story. On Girard's reading, that is the revelation settled into ordinary language.

303fact
The Athenian pharmakos rite had no accusing sentence in it; the man led out of the city was called a purification, not an injustice.
Valerius Harpocration, Lexicon in decem oratores Atticos, ed. W. Dindorf (Oxford, 1853), s.v. Φαρμακός (Greek text on Greek Wikisource)
letter Φ, entry Φαρμακός
Read the passage
What it does not say
Whether the historical Athenian pharmakos was killed or only expelled is disputed (parts-08-09.md, 8.7/8.8): the ancient testimony read here says 'led out', not killed. The script's claim here is only that the rite carried no accusing sentence, which this text supports without resolving the killed/expelled dispute.

Now the consequence Girard drew, and it is the darker half. The scapegoat mechanism works only while it is invisible. In his words, "Having a scapegoat means not knowing that we have one".304 Once a society knows what scapegoating is, its crowds can no longer find the unanimous, untroubled peace the old crowds found. Someone will always say that the victim was innocent. And the cost of that knowledge is high. "Learning that we have a scapegoat is to lose it forever and to expose ourselves to mimetic conflicts with no possible resolution". That sentence is worth holding on to. The revelation does not cure the rivalry that made the scapegoat necessary. People still copy each other's desires, still become doubles, still feud. What the revelation removes is the old way of ending it. It has not prevented the worst. The century that coined the word genocide persecuted Europe's Jews with every old sign of the scapegoat, organised by a state on an industrial scale.305

Girard: having a scapegoat means not knowing we have one; learning we have one is to lose it forever and expose us to mimetic conflicts with no resolution.
René Girard, 'On War and Apocalypse', First Things, August 2009 (adapted from material of Battling to the End)

So rivalry finds a new form, and it is the form most familiar to anyone who has spent an evening online. A crowd forms around a target. The target is said to have done something unforgivable, usually a remark. Thousands of people who have never met him join in, each copying the anger of the last. His job goes, his name becomes a byword, and the crowd moves on, calmer, for a while. Check that scene against the four marks of the plague poem. A crisis: a society anxious and divided. A crime: an offence said to threaten the whole moral order. A victim chosen by marks: someone prominent, or odd, or simply visible at the wrong moment. And the violence, now social rather than physical: expulsion from work, from friendship, from public life.

The one thing that has changed is the crowd's description of itself. The old crowd said it was purifying the city. The new crowd says it is defending victims. The target is cast as an oppressor, and joining in is cast as protecting the weak. Girard saw this coming and gave it a severe name. He spoke of a new totalitarianism, one that, in his words, "presents itself as the liberator of humanity".306 Persecution in the name of the persecuted is the old mechanism wearing the revelation as a disguise. That bears on Nietzsche's charge: a story that sides with victims can feed one more crowd. His deeper charge, that the story curses life, Girard never fully answered. A guard is needed here, because this point is often used as a weapon by one side of politics against the other. The mechanism has no side. Every faction claims the victim's mantle and uses it against its own nearest rival. That is what doubles would do. The test is never which side a crowd is on. It is whether the crowd is unanimous and its target chosen by marks. And it is whether anyone in it can still be heard saying the target might be innocent. A Massachusetts minister once said it about ten suspected witches, and a court listened.307

306quotation
Girard named a new totalitarianism that presents itself as the liberator of humanity.
René Girard, I See Satan Fall Like Lightning (Orbis, 2001), ch. 14 'The Twofold Nietzschean Heritage'
ch. 14, "The Twofold Nietzschean Heritage", p. 180

Now the larger danger. If the old cure is gone and rivalry remains, something must stop rivalry between whole nations. Girard found his answer in an unexpected place: a Prussian general's book on war, written two centuries ago. The general, Carl von Clausewitz, described war as a contest in which each side must answer the other's last move. If one side raises its effort, the other must raise its own, or lose. "He will dictate the law to me as I did to him", Clausewitz wrote. And so "there arises a sort of reciprocal action, which in the conception, must lead to an extreme".308 In the conception means in pure logic. Clausewitz himself said real wars are held back by politics and friction, and Girard took the pure logic as the truth about rivalry.309 That is the mechanism of doubles, written by a soldier about armies. Each side copies the other's escalation. Neither can stop first. Girard drew the conclusion: "Without realizing it, Clausewitz discovered not only the apocalyptic formula but also that it is bound up with mimetic rivalry".310 The formula, in plain words, is escalation with no limit. And he added the fact that makes it urgent. "Ours is the first society that knows it can completely destroy itself".311

308quotation
Clausewitz described war as a reciprocal action in which each side must match the other's escalation, in pure logic leading to an extreme.
Carl von Clausewitz, On War, trans. J. J. Graham (1873), Book I ch. 1
Bk I ch. 1, §4

Here is the objection, and it is backed by numbers. Violence has not risen. It has fallen, enormously. Most people assume the past was about as violent as the present, give or take a war. The best long series on European homicide says otherwise. In the late Middle Ages, estimates run between twenty and forty killings a year for every hundred thousand people. By the middle of the twentieth century the figure was between half a killing and one. That is a fall of thirty times or more. The historian who assembled the series warned that his figures are not precise measurements, but judged the decline real and notable.312 On this view Girard's apocalyptic warnings are simply wrong about the direction of history.

312statistic
European homicide estimates run 20-40 per 100,000 in the late Middle Ages, falling to 0.5-1 per 100,000 by the mid-20th century, a decline the historian who assembled the series called real and notable though not precise measurement.
Manuel Eisner, 'Modernization, Self-Control and Lethal Violence: The Long-term Dynamics of European Homicide Rates in Theoretical Perspective', British Journal of Criminology 41 (2001): 618-638
p. 628, section 'The Main Patterns and Secular Trends'
Read the passage
Manuel Eisner, 'Modernization, Self-Control and Lethal Violence', British Journal of Criminology 41 (2001)
p. 628
Read the passage
How it holds
The '30 times or more' fall is the script's own arithmetic from the two ranges given on the same page.
What it does not say
Eisner's own hedge ('should not be regarded as precise measurements') should accompany the figure; the data are homicide only, Europe only, from court and coroner records, not war deaths. A published critique (Schwerhoff et al. 2021) exists and was not read; do not call the numbers uncontested. The link is a copy of the article as published in the British Journal of Criminology, kept on a third-party academic site; no copy on the journal's or author's own site was reachable.

That objection deserves a careful answer, and part of it should be conceded. First, the decline fits Girard's own account at the level of ordinary life. Courts end feuds, as shown earlier. A culture that sides with victims hesitates to lynch. Both are on the side of falling homicide. The theory has no quarrel with the numbers. Second, the homicide series measures killings between neighbours, not escalation between states. The writer most associated with the decline has himself said he is not predicting that large wars will never return.313 Girard's warning was about that remaining capacity at the top, where nations face each other as doubles with weapons that can end everything. And third, what has to be granted. Girard's tone outran his evidence. He wrote as if catastrophe were close, and nothing measured shows that it is. So the ruling is this. Everyday violence has fallen, which fits his account of courts, though he did not predict it. The escalation he feared remains possible, as his theory also says, and there is no automatic brake on it. Whether it happens is no longer settled by any mechanism. It is settled by choices.

313quotation
The homicide decline measures killings between neighbours, not escalation between states; the writer most associated with the decline has said he is not predicting large wars will never return.
Steven Pinker, 'Frequently Asked Questions about The Better Angels of Our Nature' (author's site)
FAQ page
Read the passage
How it holds
The 'measures killings between neighbours, not escalation between states' half is the Eisner source's own 'what is measured' note.
What it does not say
The page is Pinker's own statement of his thesis on his own site.

Girard's own statement of the choice was blunt. "Abstain completely from retaliation and renounce the trend to extremes".314 Clausewitz's own line is the obvious reply: the side that stops first gets dictated to. That is true, and it is the price. The alternative is two sides each waiting for the other to stop, which has no end at all. It is not a defence policy. It describes the only door out of a mirror. If two doubles cannot be stopped from outside, one of them has to stop copying.

314quotation
Girard's own statement of the choice: abstain completely from retaliation and renounce the trend to extremes.
René Girard, 'On War and Apocalypse', First Things, August 2009
online text
Read the passage
What it does not say
The link is the essay as First Things published it; that is its first publication, so the page is Girard's own text, not a summary of it.

So the modern situation, on Girard's account, looks like this. No society has ever cared about victims as ours does, and our language now names the scapegoat as an injustice. That has spoiled the old cure, without taking away the rivalry it cured. The rivalry now dresses its crowds in the victim's name, and between nations it can still climb to extremes. Violence has fallen, but the sacred brake is gone, and the choice to stop has passed to us. Which leaves the most practical question of all. If copying cannot be switched off, then stopping cannot mean ceasing to imitate. It has to mean imitating something else.

Part 16: Choose the Model

Imitation cannot be switched off. Every desire beyond appetite comes from a model, and nobody chooses to stop having models. But the direction of imitation can be chosen. A person can copy a model who cannot become a rival. And a person can refuse to copy a crowd. That is the last claim. The same triangle that breeds rivalry and violence becomes, with a different model at its third corner, the one that frees.

The first case is a stoning that was not stopped, told by a writer who approved of it. An ancient life of a famous holy man, Apollonius, tells how a plague came "to rage in Ephesus, and no remedy sufficed to check" it.315 The holy man gathered the people at the theatre and pointed to an old beggar in rags.316 The writer says he was only pretending to be blind.317 "Pick up as many stones as you can and hurl them at this enemy of the gods", he told them.318 The crowd balked. They were "shocked at the idea of murdering a stranger so manifestly miserable", and the man was begging them for mercy.319 Apollonius "insisted and egged on the Ephesians".320 Then the scene turns on one line. "And as soon as some of them began to take shots and hit him with their stones", the beggar showed eyes "full of fire". Only then did the crowd decide what he was: "Then the Ephesians recognised that he was a demon".321 They buried him under a heap of stones.322 When the heap was lifted, the writer says, the body was a huge beast like a Molossian dog.323 A statue of "the Averting god, namely Hercules" was set up on the spot, and the city was declared purged of the plague.324 The marks are there: a plague, a stranger, a beggar, a killing. The victim is blamed for the plague, and the cure is credited to a god, though here not to the victim himself. And there is the hardest moment in any stoning, recorded by a writer who wanted it done. The crowd would not throw until someone threw. The writer gives the frenzy a supernatural cause, the fire in the beggar's eyes. But the fire appears only after some had begun, and in his own account the decent refusal broke at the first stones. Girard set this story beside the next one, and noted that at first "these good people refused to do it".325

Now the scene in which a crowd is stopped, because it shows where the mechanism can be broken. A woman is brought before Jesus, accused of adultery. The law, her accusers say, commands that she be stoned. The crowd is unanimous. There is no plague here and no victim picked by signs, but the stoning itself is ready, except the throw. He answers with one sentence. "He that is without sin among you, let him first cast a stone at her".326 And then, the story says, they "went out one by one, beginning at the eldest, even unto the last".327

John 8:7

The key word in that sentence is first. The old law of stoning already knew how a stoning starts. It required that "the hands of the witnesses shall be first upon him", and only afterward the hands of all the people.328 Someone throws first, and everyone else copies. Girard saw why that first throw matters so much. "The first stone is the most difficult because there is no model to imitate".329 Every stone after it is easy. The tenth thrower is only doing what nine people have already shown him how to do. So the sentence aims at the one throw a crowd cannot do without. It asks each person to be the first, alone, with no one to copy, and on condition of being without sin. Nobody can meet the condition. Nobody throws.

328quotation
The old law of stoning required that the hands of the witnesses be first upon the condemned, and only afterward the hands of all the people.
The Holy Bible, King James Version, Deuteronomy
Deuteronomy 17:7

329quotation
Girard: the first stone is the most difficult to throw because there is no model to imitate.
René Girard, 'The First Stone', Renascence 52:1 (1999), reposted as 'Casting the First Stone - by René Girard'
essay, Apollonius section
Read the passage
How it holds
An earlier research pass had left this claim BLOCKED (wording known only from secondary summaries); a later curl-based re-source session reached the essay through Wayback and confirmed this exact wording.
What it does not say
Read via a Wayback Machine capture of mimetictheory.com (the live page returns 403 to the fetch tool); mimetictheory.com is not in the tier file.

Now look at how the crowd leaves. One by one, beginning with the eldest. The leaving is copied too. The oldest man, perhaps the one with the most to remember, puts his stone down and goes. The next one follows him. A crowd that would have copied the first stone copies the first refusal instead. The mechanism did not change. What changed was the model. Not every reader accepts this reading of the scene, and it should be held as Girard's interpretation rather than as the text's plain meaning. But the old law's own words make the point about the first throw, and that point stands without him.

Because refusal can be copied too, one person declining to throw can turn a crowd around. The first stone and the first refusal are the same choice, made in opposite directions.

The second half of the claim is about the model a person chooses. The comparison from the very start, between the knight copying Amadis and the Christian copying Christ. Girard meant it literally.330 Both are far models. A far model cannot become a rival, because he does not reach for the things his follower reaches for. There is no promotion that both want, no seat that only one can have. Copying such a model never turns the model into an obstacle. That is why the Christian tradition puts so much weight on imitation. One of its earliest letters says, "Be ye followers of me, even as I also am of Christ".331 And it is why Girard called Christ "the only model, the one that places man at just the right distance from the divine". The right distance is the one at which imitation cannot turn into rivalry. He added the condition that makes it work: "We can all participate in the divinity of Christ so long as we renounce our own violence".332

What it does not say
The link is the essay as First Things published it; that is its first publication, so the page is Girard's own text, not a summary of it.

A listener who does not share that faith is owed an honest account of how much of this they can keep. Girard's own answer was Christ, and for him no other model would do. But the structure of his argument does not depend on the faith. Distance alone is not enough, and the knight proves it. Amadis was far, and copying him still wrecked a life. What separates a safe model from a ruinous one is what the model wants. Amadis wanted glory, and glory shrinks when it is shared, so his follower went looking for every fight he could find. Some goods do not shrink when shared: a skill, an understanding, a refusal to hate. A master who wants the work done well, or a teacher who wants the student to surpass him, is far less likely to become a rival. Such a model can be copied by many people at once, and nobody takes anything from anybody.

The dying knight shows what giving up a ruinous model like Amadis looks like. On his deathbed he gives up the books and the borrowed name. "I am no longer Don Quixote of La Mancha, but Alonso Quixano, whose way of life won for him the name of Good".333 Girard read the deathbeds of the great novels as one scene repeated.334 The hero gives up the model he had made into a god, and in doing so gives up his chains. "In renouncing divinity the hero renounces slavery".335 What the dying knight renounces is not imitation. He renounces a model he had raised into an idol, whose desires ruled his life. He takes back his own name, and the name he takes back is the one he earned by being good to the people near him.

This is the line the script's whole 'choose the model' argument turns on.
What it does not say
Read in a digital copy of this printed edition; it is not linked because the free copies online are unauthorised uploads of an in-copyright book. So here is what all this gives a person to do, in three plain tests. The first is for a desire. When something suddenly seems necessary, ask whose desire showed it to you, and whether you would want it if nobody you watch wanted it. Most of the time there will be a model. Knowing it is the difference between Quixote, who chose his openly, and Emma, who never knew she had one. The second is for an enemy. When someone gets under your skin, ask what the two of you both want. Then ask how alike you have become in the fight. The enemy who seems your opposite is very often your double. And recall the mother before the king's sword. Between doubles, the fight ends when one of them is willing to let go of the thing, and means it. The third is for a crowd. When a crowd forms around a target, ask whether you know anything about the target that you did not learn from the crowd. Ask who threw first. And ask whether you are about to be the second. A jury in Salem had the right answer once, and handed it back to the crowd's outcry.336

336fact
A jury in Salem returned the right verdict once and had it overturned by the crowd's outcry.
Robert Calef, More Wonders of the Invisible World (London, 1700), Part V, in George Lincoln Burr (ed.), Narratives of the Witchcraft Cases, 1648-1706 (Scribner, 1914)
p. 358 (leaf 384)
Read the passage
Robert Calef, More Wonders of the Invisible World (London, 1700), Part V, in George Lincoln Burr (ed.), Narratives of the Witchcraft Cases, 1648-1706 (Scribner, 1914)
p. 358 (leaf 384)
Read the passage
What it does not say
Callback to Part 14's fuller account of Rebecca Nurse's trial (same source, extend-salem.md).

Now the whole argument, from the first jacket to the last stone, without the cases. Beyond appetite, desire is copied from a model. A near model becomes a rival, and the copier hides his admiration as contempt. Desire at its deepest wants the model's being, so the won prize goes flat. Rivals copy each other until the object fades and they become doubles, and violence spreads by copying. In a group, rivalry dissolves the differences that kept people apart, and the crisis has no reliable brake. Then the crisis turns into all against one, in the checked cases where fear, an old accusation and a marked group met, with power helping. On Girard's account the killing brings a peace. The crowd blames the victim for the crisis and credits him with the peace, and he becomes sacred. Later tellings soften the killing, and where the text keeps its traces, the softening can be caught. Rules, rites and sacrifice keep that peace, and courts inherit the work. Then the stories change sides. The Hebrew Bible keeps the plot of the myth and reverses its verdict in story after story. The best tragedy saw the crowd and still left the victim guilty. The Passion shows the whole mechanism from the victim's side, and a mechanism that needs to be unseen loses its innocence once it is seen. One Christian town, within a generation, said in writing that it had been deluded. The concern for victims takes away the old cure without ending rivalry, so the choice to stop passes to us. And imitation cannot stop, but its model can be chosen, and refusing the first stone is a model too.

The darkest objection from the start, that a borrowed desire is not really yours, now has its full answer. Borrowed is the only kind of desire there is. Freedom was never going to be found in having no model. It lies in knowing which model you have, and in choosing one whose desire leaves room for yours and for everyone else's.

So this is what stands at the end. The romantic lie said desire runs straight from a self to a thing. It runs through other people, always. That is why the people nearest us become our rivals, and why rivals become doubles. It is why crowds turn on one, and why that one was once called a god. On Girard's reading, a story told from the victim's side broke that circle's power to hide itself, and left the choice with us. The next time you want something badly, or hate someone closely, or feel a crowd pulling you toward a target, one question is left. It is whose desire this is, and whether that is the model you would choose.
